# EQ Architecture Roadmap and Stage Record — Consolidated

**Document Authority:** Authoritative Project-Stage Tracker & Architecture Specification for Node Type EQ  
**Governance Hierarchy:** Global Rules MD $\to$ EQ Rules & Roadmap MD $\to$ Implementation (ESPHome YAML & Native C/C++)  
**Current Status:** **Stage 0: COMPLETE** | **Stage 1: COMPLETE** | **Stage 2: READY TO START**  
**Target Hardware:** ESP32 Family; ESPHome runtime framework with custom native C/C++ core engines  

---

# 1. Document Authority & Stage Gate Record

This document is the authoritative architectural baseline and stage record for the **Equalizer (EQ)** node type.

### Current Position: Stage 1 Formal Closure
* **Stage 0 — EQ Node Baseline & Reference Freeze:** **COMPLETE** (Legacy behavior documented and frozen as reference evidence).
* **Stage 1 — EQ Subsystem Architecture Definition:** **COMPLETE** (All 14 subsystem domains, boundaries, authority models, safety invariants, error lifecycles, and cross-node contracts are architecturally locked).
* **Stage 2 — Resource & Provider Engine Implementation:** **READY TO START** (Active handoff point).

### Stage 1 Exit Sign-Off & Handoff Matrix
Implementation-specific engineering details are assigned to their designated downstream stages:

```text
┌───────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
│ ARCHITECTURAL CONTRACT (STAGE 1 LOCKED)│ IMPLEMENTATION HOME (STAGES 2–11)     │
├───────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Resource Arbitration & Data Quality   │ Stage 2: Provider Drivers & Buffers   │
│ Transactional Flash & Reset Scopes    │ Stage 3: NVS Structs & CRC Layouts    │
│ State Machine & Fault Latches         │ Stage 4: Central State Struct & Enums │
│ Canonical Command Pipeline            │ Stage 5: FreeRTOS Command Deserializer│
│ Monotonic Timers & Task Schedulers    │ Stage 6: FreeRTOS Core 1 Task Loops   │
│ Control Loops, Drivers & Local OM Code│ Stage 7: C++ Control & OM Code Tables │
│ Platform GPIO & Bus Allocations       │ Stage 8: ESPHome Platform Components  │
│ External Transport Wire Encoding      │ Stage 9: Modbus/BLE/REST Frame Packers│
│ Live `/status` Web Server & Streaming │ Stage 10: Async Web Handlers & Tracing│
│ Automated 3-Tier Verification Suite   │ Stage 11: Unit, Integration & HW Tests│
└───────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---

# 2. Architecture Governance & Structural Principles

## 2.1 The Four Cardinal Separations

```text
┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐
│  CONFIGURATION   │   ≠   │  RUNTIME STATE   │   ≠   │     COMMAND      │   ≠   │      EVENT       │
│  Durable Intent  │       │What Is Happening │       │   Instruction    │       │Discrete Incident │
└──────────────────┘       └──────────────────┘       └──────────────────┘       └──────────────────┘
```

1. **Configuration $\neq$ Runtime State:** Configuration expresses durable intent stored in flash. Runtime state represents volatile operating reality. Live sensor values or dynamic control targets must never be written to flash configuration.
2. **Command $\neq$ State:** A command is an ephemeral request. State is the authoritative condition committed by the owning service. Interfaces never write directly to state variables.
3. **Event $\neq$ State:** Events are discrete transition notifications. State is continuous truth. Reconnecting nodes must never reconstruct current state by replaying past events.
4. **Logical Policy $\neq$ Driver Mechanics:** Control engines generate unitless or normalized targets ($0\text{--}100\%$). Actuator drivers manage step intervals, PWM duty cycles, attach/detach routines, and settling delays.

## 2.2 Specification Format: Decisions vs. Rules with Edge Scenarios

To eliminate ambiguity during implementation, architectural specifications are structured with:
* **The Decision:** The technical choice made among competing alternatives.
* **The Normative Rule:** The operational law enforced in code.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Expected execution under ideal nominal conditions.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Edge cases, bus faults, dropouts, or race conditions demonstrating why the rule protects the system.

---

# 3. EQ Node Definition & Ownership Model

## 3.1 Replicable node architecture

EQ is a replicable node type (`EQ-001`, `EQ-002`, $\dots$, `EQ-N`). Instances share an identical codebase; differences are managed via runtime configuration, bus address assignments, and calibration tables.

## 3.2 Autonomy invariant

```text
HUB DISCONNECTED / NETWORK COLLAPSE
                 ↓
EQ NODE CONTINUES 100% UNINTERRUPTED
    ├── Local Sensing (Internal/External Temp & Humidity)
    ├── Climate Equilibrium Control Loops
    ├── Local Safety Interlocks & Emergency Detach
    ├── Fan PWM & Servo Flap Actuation
    ├── Flash Storage of Committed Setpoint Changes
    └── Local Diagnostics & Fault Latching
```

The Hub provides coordination and dashboard integration; it is not the control brain. When the Hub goes offline, the EQ continues autonomous regulation.

## 3.3 Subsystem ownership matrix

```text
┌──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
│ DOMAIN / FACT            │ AUTHORITATIVE OWNER      │ HUB PERMISSION           │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
│ Node Identity & Network  │ Local Node Core          │ Read-Only                │
│ Operating Configuration  │ Local Configuration Core │ Request Change (via NVS) │
│ Logical Resources        │ Resource Engine          │ Read-Only (Queryable)    │
│ Operating Mode           │ Mode & Authority Manager │ Request Transition       │
│ Actuator Safety State    │ Operational Matrix (OM)  │ Read-Only                │
│ Target Generation        │ Climate Control Engine   │ Read-Only                │
│ Physical Fan Output      │ Fan Driver               │ Zero Direct Control      │
│ Physical Flap Output     │ Flap Servo Driver        │ Zero Direct Control      │
│ Active Faults & Latches  │ Fault Engine             │ Clear-Fault Request Only │
└──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘
```

---

# 4. Mode & Control-Authority Management

## 4.1 Whole-device operating modes

EQ operates in one of five mutually exclusive system modes:
1. **`OFF`:** Normal climate control is suspended. Sensing, safety watchdogs, communications, and fault logging remain active. Actuators remain in safe closed positions.
2. **`AUTO/TEMPERATURE`:** Climate engine regulates ventilation to drive indoor temperature toward outdoor temperature within configured deadbands.
3. **`AUTO/HUMIDITY`:** Climate engine regulates ventilation to drive indoor relative humidity toward outdoor humidity within configured deadbands.
4. **`MANUAL`:** Autonomous climate loops are disengaged. The fan target is set directly by user input, subject to safety gates.
5. **`COMPLIANCE`:** EQ operates under an inter-node coordination agreement requested by the Thermostat (HVAC), running a dedicated purge cycle to assist environmental conditioning.

*Note: `FAULT` is a runtime health condition, not an operating mode.*

```text
                              ┌──────────────┐
            ┌─────────────────┤     OFF      ├────────────────┐
            │                 └──────┬───────┘                │
            │                        │                        │
            ▼                        ▼                        ▼
     ┌──────────────┐         ┌──────────────┐         ┌──────────────┐
     │    MANUAL    │◄───────►│     AUTO     │◄───────►│  COMPLIANCE  │
     └──────────────┘         └──┬────────┬──┘         └──────────────┘
                                 │        │
                     ┌───────────┘        └───────────┐
                     ▼                                ▼
            ┌──────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────┐
            │ AUTO/TEMPERATURE │            │  AUTO/HUMIDITY   │
            └──────────────────┘            └──────────────────┘
```

## 4.2 Subsystem decisions & operational rules

### MCA-01: Whole-Device Mode vs. Actuator Domain Authority
* **The Decision:** System operating mode represents whole-device autonomy; control authority is resolved independently per physical actuator domain (Fan Domain, Flap Domain).
* **The Rule:** An operating mode change does not grant an external caller direct ownership of an actuator. Actuator targets are derived from the active domain authority.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** System transitions to `AUTO/TEMPERATURE`. The climate engine generates a $50\%$ fan target. The Fan authority routes this to the driver, which moves the fan to $50\%$.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A user sets `MANUAL` mode with a fan target of $80\%$, while an active safety interlock detects an obstructed intake. The device mode remains `MANUAL`, but Safety assumes authority over the Fan domain, forcing the effective target to $0\%$.

### MCA-02: Deterministic Authority Precedence
* **The Decision:** Actuator authority conflicts are resolved by fixed precedence rather than network arrival order or callback timing:
  $$\text{SAFETY} > \text{CALIBRATION} > \text{COMPLIANCE} > \text{MANUAL} > \text{AUTO} > \text{DEFAULT/OFF}$$
* **The Rule:** Lower-priority requests are held in memory or rejected; they cannot preempt higher-priority authorities. When a higher authority releases, the system deterministically falls back to the highest remaining valid request.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** EQ runs in `AUTO/TEMPERATURE` ($35\%$ fan). A user requests manual override to $70\%$. Authority transfers to `MANUAL`, setting the fan to $70\%$. When the manual session expires, authority reverts cleanly to `AUTO`, resuming dynamic tracking.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A mobile app sends an `AUTO` command at the same millisecond an emergency stop triggers. Regardless of FreeRTOS task scheduling or packet race conditions, Safety evaluates first in the Operational Matrix, forcing an immediate actuator halt and rejecting the Auto target.

### MCA-03: Implicit Submode Selection via Setpoints
* **The Decision:** Writing a temperature setpoint implicitly selects `AUTO/TEMPERATURE`. Writing a humidity setpoint implicitly selects `AUTO/HUMIDITY`.
* **The Rule:** The Command Manager updates the respective configuration parameter and commands a mode transition within a single atomic transaction.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** EQ is in `MANUAL`. A user adjusts the target temperature on the wall display to $22^\circ\text{C}$. The setpoint updates in NVS and the device transitions to `AUTO/TEMPERATURE` without requiring a separate mode command.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A user rapidly alternates between temperature and humidity adjustments on a high-latency connection. Because transactions are atomic and serialized through the Command Manager, the last validated transaction sets the mode without leaving the system in an ambiguous dual-mode state.

---

# 5. Fan Control Subsystem

## 5.1 Subsystem boundaries

The Fan Control Engine owns target generation, response profiles, ramp generation, feedback validation, calibration sequencing, and shutdown delays. The physical Fan Driver owns PWM timers, relay GPIOs, tachometer edge counting, and current sense ADCs.

```text
Logical Target (0–100%)
          ↓
Operational Matrix Gate (Safety & Lockout Checks)
          ↓
Ramp Profiler (Configurable %/sec Slew Rate)
          ↓
Physical PWM Mapping (Floor to Ceiling Mapping)
          ↓
Startup Sequence Handler (Flap Check → Relay ON → 500ms Settle → Boost)
          ↓
Hardware Actuation (PWM Pin & Relay Pin)
```

## 5.2 Fan control decisions & operational rules

### FAN-01: Logical 0–100% Target to Physical PWM Bounds
* **The Decision:** User and control interfaces reason strictly in logical $0\text{--}100\%$. The physical driver maps this across persisted minimum and maximum physical PWM limits.
* **The Rule:** Logical $0\%$ always corresponds to physical power removal via relay. Any positive logical target ($1\text{--}100\%$) is mapped linearly across the usable physical PWM range:
  $$\text{PWM}_{\text{out}} = \text{PWM}_{\text{min}} + \left(\frac{\text{Target}_{\text{log}}}{100} \times (\text{PWM}_{\text{max}} - \text{PWM}_{\text{min}})\right)$$
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Configured physical limits are $30\%$ (min) and $100\%$ (max). Control loop requests $50\%$. Driver scales this to $65\%$ PWM duty cycle, driving the fan at mid-scale.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An out-of-spec motor stalls below $45\%$ PWM. An operator reconfigures $\text{PWM}_{\text{min}}$ to $45\%$. A subsequent low-speed request of $1\%$ maps to $45.55\%$ physical PWM, keeping the motor above its stall threshold while user logic continues using standard percentages.

### FAN-02: Startup Boost & Relay Settling Sequence
* **The Decision:** Every start from a stopped state executes a staged relay settling and dynamic low-speed boost routine.
* **The Rule:** Starting from $0\%$ requires:
  1. Confirm Flap satisfies the minimum open condition.
  2. Engage power relay; pause $500\text{ ms}$ for inrush stabilization.
  3. If logical target is $>0\%$ and $<20\%$, apply a $20\%$ boost PWM for $2.0\text{ seconds}$ to overcome static friction.
  4. Slew to target and evaluate feedback.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Controller requests $5\%$ fan speed. The flap opens, the relay activates, and after $500\text{ ms}$ the driver applies $20\%$ boost for $2\text{ seconds}$. The motor starts spinning smoothly, after which the driver ramps down to the $5\%$ target.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Controller requests $5\%$ in cold ambient conditions with bearing friction. Without boost, the motor draws stall current without rotating. With boost, static friction is broken; if rotation is still not detected after $2\text{ seconds}$, the feedback monitor aborts the start and enters a controlled retry sequence.

### FAN-03: Triple-Evaluation Feedback & Disagreement Diagnostics
* **The Decision:** Tachometer RPM and Current Sensing are treated as independent logical resources. Validation failure requires three consecutive failed evaluation windows.
* **The Rule:** Samples are gathered over discrete measurement windows. A single anomalous sample is ignored. Three consecutive failed evaluations latch a structured `FAN_FAULT`. When both sensors are present, physical disagreement (e.g., normal current with zero RPM) triggers a dedicated diagnostic flag.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** The fan runs at $70\%$. Electrical noise causes a single spurious tachometer pulse drop. The filter flags evaluation 1 as suspect. The next two evaluations return normal readings; the counter resets to zero with no disruption.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** The fan belt snaps while running under load. Tachometer drops to $0\text{ RPM}$ while the motor continues drawing no-load current. Over three consecutive evaluation cycles, RPM remains zero while current is detected. The driver latches a `TACH_DISAGREEMENT_FAULT`, shuts down the power relay, and reports the failure.

### FAN-04: Controlled Shutdown Sequence & Restart Delay
* **The Decision:** Fan shutdown uses a staged ramp-down and power-isolation sequence, with a mandatory restart lockout timer.
* **The Rule:** Normal stop requires:
  1. Ramp PWM to $0\%$.
  2. Confirm physical stop via feedback (or timeout if feedback unavailable).
  3. Maintain relay power for a $10\text{-second}$ power-down window.
  4. Disengage relay.
  5. Start the monotonic restart lockout timer from actual relay de-energization.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A stop command is received. The driver ramps PWM to $0\%$, detects motor halt via tachometer, waits $10\text{ seconds}$ for thermal dissipation, turns off the relay, and starts the restart timer.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A user rapidly toggles the fan `ON` $\to$ `OFF` $\to$ `ON` via a physical switch. During the $10\text{-second}$ power-down window, the new `ON` request reuses the energized path without cycling the relay. If the toggle occurs after relay de-energization, the restart timer blocks immediate re-energization, protecting the relay contacts from arcing.

### FAN-05: Atomic Multi-Point Calibration Sequence
* **The Decision:** Field calibration uses an atomic 6-state machine (`IDLE` $\to$ `PREPARING` $\to$ `RUNNING` $\to$ `COMPLETE` / `FAILED` / `CANCELLED`).
* **The Rule:** Calibration measures physical PWM, average RPM, and average current across deterministic steps ($0, N, 2N, \dots, 100\%$). Each step allows up to 3 stabilization retries. Failure of any required point aborts the calibration, discards the working buffer, and retains the previous valid calibration table in flash.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A technician initiates calibration with step size $20\%$. Points $0, 20, 40, 60, 80, 100\%$ settle cleanly on attempt 1. Data is validated, committed to flash atomically, and updated in the active control loop.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An external wind gust destabilizes the fan at the $60\%$ calibration point. Attempts 1 and 2 fail the stability threshold. Attempt 3 also fails. The calibration aborts to `FAILED`, the partial data is discarded, and the prior calibration table remains active.

---

# 6. Flap (Vent) Control Subsystem

## 6.1 Subsystem boundaries

The Flap Control Engine calculates logical target positions based on fan speed curves or manual commands. The physical Flap Driver translates logical percentage into physical servo angles, manages step-rate limiting, handles attach/detach routines, and executes power-down settling.

```text
Logical Flap Target (0–100%)
          ↓
Physical Driver Bounds Mapping & Inversion
          ↓
Rate Limiter (50ms interval, 1% step size)
          ↓
Servo PWM Driver (Physical Angle Control)
          ↓
Position Settling Timer (5.0s Hold at Target)
          ↓
Driver Detach (Power removed from motor coils)
```

## 6.2 Flap control decisions & operational rules

### FLP-01: Logical 0–100% Position with Physical Inversion
* **The Decision:** High-level software treats $0\%$ as fully closed and $100\%$ as fully open. The physical driver maps this range to physical servo pulse widths and handles directional inversion.
* **The Rule:** Inversion flips physical endpoints without changing logical semantics. $0\%$ always means aerodynamically closed; $100\%$ always means aerodynamically open.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Standard mounting maps $0\%\to 1.0\text{ ms}$ pulse width and $100\%\to 2.0\text{ ms}$. Flap closes cleanly when $0\%$ is commanded.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Inverted physical linkage requires $2.0\text{ ms}$ for closed and $1.0\text{ ms}$ for open. Setting `inversion = TRUE` flips the pulse mappings in the driver. Control algorithms continue sending $0\%$ for closed and $100\%$ for open with no logic changes.

### FLP-02: Configurable Multi-Point Fan $\to$ Flap Curve
* **The Decision:** Automatic flap positioning uses a configurable, monotonic non-decreasing $2\text{--}20$ point curve mapping Fan Target % to Flap Target %.
* **The Rule:** Curve constraints:
  * Point 1 defines the minimum fan speed and required flap opening.
  * Intermediate points use linear interpolation.
  * Monotonically non-decreasing: point $N+1$ flap target cannot be lower than point $N$.
  * Updates are atomic; invalid curves are rejected in their entirety.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Valid curve: Point 1 ($10\%\text{ fan} \to 20\%\text{ flap}$), Point 2 ($50\%\text{ fan} \to 60\%\text{ flap}$), Point 3 ($100\%\text{ fan} \to 100\%\text{ flap}$). Fan commands scale smoothly across the profile.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A user uploads a 5-point curve where Point 4 commands a lower flap opening than Point 3. The configuration validator rejects the transaction, logs a validation error, and keeps the existing curve active.

### FLP-03: Timed Settling and Coil Detach Execution
* **The Decision:** To prevent servo motor burnout, hum, and unnecessary power draw, servo PWM is detached during stationary periods.
* **The Rule:** Movement execution:
  1. If target is within logical tolerance ($1\%$), take no action.
  2. Attach servo PWM driver.
  3. Step to target at configured slew rate ($50\text{ ms}$ step, $1\%$ increment).
  4. When target is reached, hold position in `SETTLING` state for $5.0\text{ seconds}$.
  5. Detach servo PWM output.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Flap moves from $20\%$ to $80\%$. Driver attaches, steps smoothly over 3 seconds, holds for $5\text{ seconds}$ to counter air resistance, then detaches. The flap holds position aerodynamically with unpowered coils.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Flap is settling at $80\%$ (second 2 of 5). A new target of $30\%$ arrives. The driver cancels the remaining settling time, stays attached, and immediately steps down toward $30\%$, preventing mechanical chatter.

### FLP-04: Logical Execution Tracking (No Phantom Physical Feedback)
* **The Decision:** The standard servo lacks physical position feedback. Firmware tracks logical commanded state and driver execution, without claiming verified physical position.
* **The Rule:** The system reports movement states: `CLOSED`, `OPENING`, `OPEN`, `CLOSING`, `SETTLING`. Telemetry explicitly flags position data as commanded, never as measured. POST verifies driver completion, not physical blade travel.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** UI displays flap status as `OPENING (Commanded: 60%)`. When stepping and settling complete, status reports `OPEN (Commanded: 60%)`.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Flap linkage jams mechanically while the servo horn rotates. Firmware reports `OPEN` based on driver stepping completion. Because the driver does not claim measured feedback, diagnostic logs accurately reflect that the driver completed its stepping sequence, guiding the technician to inspect the mechanical linkage rather than chasing a phantom sensor fault.

---

# 7. Fan ↔ Flap Safety Invariant & Takeover

## 7.1 The fundamental safety invariant

$$\mathbf{FAN\_RUNNING} \implies \mathbf{FLAP\_SATISFIES\_REQUIRED\_MINIMUM\_OPENING}$$

The ventilation fan must never operate against a closed flap. Airflow restriction causes motor overheating, pressure imbalances, and duct damage.

## 7.2 Safety invariant decisions & operational rules

### FFI-01: Fan Startup Waits on Flap Prerequisite
* **The Decision:** Fan startup sequence halts execution until the Flap Driver confirms the flap has reached or exceeded the minimum required opening defined by Curve Point 1.
* **The Rule:** The Fan relay cannot close, and PWM cannot rise above $0\%$, until Flap Driver execution moves the flap to the safe threshold.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Fan start commanded. Flap Driver attaches and steps to $20\%$ open. Flap reaches position; Fan Driver then energizes the relay and starts the fan ramp.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Servo power fails during flap opening. Flap Driver never completes stepping to the safe position. The Fan Driver times out waiting for the flap prerequisite, cancels startup, leaves the relay open, and logs a `FLAP_INTERLOCK_BLOCKED` fault.

### FFI-02: Flap Closure Blocks on Physical Fan Halt
* **The Decision:** The Flap Driver will not close the flap below the minimum safety threshold while the fan is energized or rotating.
* **The Rule:** A flap close command is held in a pending state until the Fan Driver confirms physical stop (via feedback or power-down timeout).
* **Best-Case Scenario:** System commands `OFF`. Fan ramps to $0\%$, spins down, and relay cuts power. Flap then moves to $0\%$ and detaches.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Fan feedback indicates bearings are free-wheeling long after power is cut. A flap close request arrives. The flap holds open until the tachometer drops to zero, protecting the ductwork from back-pressure spikes.

### FFI-03: Manual Flap Close Takeover Sequence
* **The Decision:** If an operator manually commands an exact $0\%$ flap closure while the fan is actively running, the system initiates a controlled shutdown takeover.
* **The Rule:** Takeover sequence:
  1. Capture the current Fan Operating Mode to volatile RAM.
  2. Transition Fan Domain authority to `MANUAL`, target $0\%$.
  3. Execute standard Fan shutdown (ramp down $\to$ stop confirm $\to$ relay off).
  4. Grant Flap Domain manual authority and step the flap to $0\%$.
  5. If manual close is cancelled, restore the saved Operating Mode.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Fan runs in `AUTO` at $60\%$. User commands Flap $0\%$ via web interface. System captures `AUTO`, ramps fan to $0\%$, turns off relay, closes flap, and reports takeover active.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Power fails mid-takeover. Because the captured mode was stored in volatile RAM, reboot runs clean POST reconciliation and re-evaluates environmental setpoints fresh, rather than restoring a stale transient takeover state.

### FFI-04: Capability-Disabled Flap Bypass
* **The Decision:** If the flap capability is explicitly disabled in configuration (e.g., bare fan installation with gravity dampers), the flap dependency is logically satisfied.
* **The Rule:** When `FLAP_CAPABILITY = DISABLED`, all servo stepping, curve lookups, and flap POST sequences are bypassed. The Fan Driver evaluates the Flap dependency as permanently satisfied.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** EQ hardware deployed without a servo flap. Flap capability is set to `DISABLED`. Fan starts immediately without waiting for servo confirmation.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Operator accidentally disables flap capability on a unit with a physical motorized damper. The Fan runs, but because capability configuration is decoupled from physical evidence, diagnostics highlight that flap actuation is bypassed, identifying the configuration error.

---

# 8. Auto Equilibrium Climate Subsystem

## 8.1 Algorithm principles

The Auto Climate Engine balances indoor climate against outdoor conditions using environmental exchange. It does not act as an air conditioner or heater; it runs ventilation only when outdoor air can move the indoor space toward the configured setpoint band.

```text
Indoor & Outdoor Sensors (Resource Engine)
                 ↓
Kelvin Error Calculation (Error = Internal - Target)
                 ↓
Outdoor Useful Delta Check (Is outdoor air on the correct side?)
                 ↓
Deadband Gate (Temperature: 0.5K, Humidity: 3%RH)
                 ↓
Proportional Target Generation (Target = Error × Response Ratio)
                 ↓
Minimum Floor Enforcement (<5% = 0%, 5–10% = 10%, ≥10% = Target)
                 ↓
Logical Fan Target Emitted to Authority Manager
```

## 8.2 Climate engine decisions & operational rules

### AUT-01: Directional Ventilation Gating
* **The Decision:** Auto ventilation runs only when outdoor air is on the corrective side of indoor air relative to the setpoint band.
* **The Rule:** Gating checks:
  * *Cooling Mode (Indoor > High Setpoint):* Requires $\text{Outdoor Temp} < \text{Indoor Temp}$. Destination clamped to $\max(\text{Low Setpoint}, \text{Outdoor Temp})$.
  * *Heating Mode (Indoor < Low Setpoint):* Requires $\text{Outdoor Temp} > \text{Indoor Temp}$. Destination clamped to $\min(\text{High Setpoint}, \text{Outdoor Temp})$.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Indoor is $28^\circ\text{C}$ (High is $25^\circ\text{C}$). Outdoor is $18^\circ\text{C}$. Outdoor air is cooler than indoor air. Ventilation runs proportionally to cool the room toward $25^\circ\text{C}$.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Indoor is $32^\circ\text{C}$ (High is $25^\circ\text{C}$). Outdoor is $38^\circ\text{C}$. Even though the room is hot, outdoor air would heat it further. The useful delta check fails; Fan Target is forced to $0\%$, preventing the system from heating the room.

### AUT-02: Floating-Point Kelvin Internal Math
* **The Decision:** All internal temperature calculations, error comparisons, and setpoint limits use floating-point Kelvin. Conversion to Celsius or Fahrenheit occurs only at user I/O boundaries.
* **The Rule:** Algorithms execute in Kelvin. Fractional precision is maintained through all stages; rounding to display resolution ($0.5^\circ$) occurs only in presentation layers.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** User sets $72^\circ\text{F}$ on the mobile app. The adapter converts this to $295.372\text{ K}$. Control math executes without precision loss, preventing rounding chatter around deadbands.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Repeated conversions between Celsius and Fahrenheit in legacy code introduced a $0.2^\circ\text{C}$ calculation drift, causing rapid fan hunting. With canonical Kelvin math, rounding errors are eliminated.

### AUT-03: Product-Life Fan Target Floor Limits
* **The Decision:** To prevent motor bearing wear and high-temperature coil degradation from prolonged low-RPM operation, calculated targets below $10\%$ follow a non-linear floor profile.
* **The Rule:** Scaling:
  * Calculated target $<5.0\% \implies \text{Effective Target} = 0\%$
  * Calculated target $\ge 5.0\%$ and $<10.0\% \implies \text{Effective Target} = 10.0\%$
  * Calculated target $\ge 10.0\% \implies \text{Effective Target} = \text{Calculated Target}$
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Environmental error generates an $18\%$ fan target. The target is above $10\%$, so it passes directly to the ramp profiler as $18\%$.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Environmental error creates a marginal $2\%$ demand. Without the floor, the motor would receive inadequate voltage, stall, and generate heat. Under the floor rule, the target snaps to $0\%$, keeping the motor safely stopped until error increases.

### AUT-04: Degraded Sensor Fallback & Manual Handover
* **The Decision:** Loss of the primary internal environmental sensor initiates a 5-minute fallback countdown, holding the last valid target before transferring authority to Manual mode.
* **The Rule:** If the controlled resource becomes `UNAVAILABLE` or `EXPIRED`:
  1. Auto transitions to `DEGRADED` state.
  2. Hold the last calculated Fan Target in RAM.
  3. Start a 5-minute monotonic fallback timer.
  4. If the sensor recovers, resume dynamic tracking.
  5. If the timer expires, transition to `MANUAL` mode with the last known target, and log an `INTERNAL_SENSOR_FAULT`.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An I2C sensor bus glitch causes temporary read timeouts. The sensor recovers after 30 seconds. The fallback timer cancels, and Auto continues tracking without interruption.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Sensor cable is severed. The 5-minute timer expires. Auto drops authority, transitioning the Fan to `MANUAL` mode at the last valid speed. The fan does not oscillate or stop abruptly; diagnostics alert the Hub of sensor loss.

---

# 9. Inter-Node HVAC Compliance Subsystem

## 9.1 Handshake contract & objective

Compliance is an inter-node operating mode where the Thermostat (HVAC node) requests that the EQ run an environmental purge to assist active heating or cooling.

```text
Thermostat Requests Compliance (HEAT | COOL)
                 ↓
EQ Validates Resource Quality & Physical Capabilities
                 ↓
EQ Evaluates Purge Delta (Is outdoor air helpful?)
                 ↓
Handshake Response Returned (ACCEPTED | NOT_USEFUL | RESOURCE_UNAVAILABLE)
                 ↓
Purge Cycle Runs (Targeting external convergence within 1.0K band)
                 ↓
Termination (Equilibrium Achieved | Max Duration Expired | Hub Loss)
                 ↓
Previous Mode & Authority Restored
```

## 9.2 Compliance decisions & operational rules

### CMP-01: Explicit Acceptance Handshake Contract
* **The Decision:** EQ validates resource health, hardware capabilities, and thermal delta before returning an acceptance status to the Thermostat.
* **The Rule:** Handshake responses:
  * `ACCEPTED`: All checks pass; purge operation is queued.
  * `NOT_USEFUL`: Request understood, but outdoor air cannot assist the requested direction (or temperatures are already equalized within the $1.0\text{ K}$ band).
  * `RESOURCE_UNAVAILABLE`: Fan or sensors in fault state, or emergency stop active.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Thermostat prepares to cool; outdoor air is $19^\circ\text{C}$, indoor is $27^\circ\text{C}$. Thermostat requests `COOL` compliance. EQ validates conditions and returns `ACCEPTED`. EQ ramps the fan to purge warm indoor air.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Thermostat requests `COOL` compliance while outdoor air is $35^\circ\text{C}$ and indoor air is $24^\circ\text{C}$. EQ rejects the request with `NOT_USEFUL`. The Thermostat receives the response and engages mechanical cooling directly without introducing hot outdoor air.

### CMP-02: Purge Execution Targets External Convergence
* **The Decision:** EQ Compliance purge targets environmental equilibrium with the current outdoor temperature, not the Thermostat's final setpoint.
* **The Rule:** The purge operation runs until:
  $$|\text{Indoor Temp} - \text{Outdoor Temp}| \le \text{Equilibrium Band } (1.0\text{ K})$$
  or the maximum duration timer expires.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Indoor air is $30^\circ\text{C}$, outdoor air is $22^\circ\text{C}$. The Thermostat targets $20^\circ\text{C}$. EQ purges until indoor reaches $23^\circ\text{C}$ ($1.0\text{ K}$ delta from outdoors), then terminates with `EQUILIBRIUM_ACHIEVED`. The Thermostat then runs its compressor to complete cooling down to $20^\circ\text{C}$.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Outdoor temperature shifts during a purge cycle (e.g., rising from $22^\circ\text{C}$ to $25^\circ\text{C}$). The EQ dynamically tracks the outdoor reference rather than a stale starting value, terminating the cycle when indoor air reaches $26^\circ\text{C}$ to prevent over-ventilation.

### CMP-03: T3 Dynamic Timer & Bounded Execution
* **The Decision:** The maximum purge duration timer starts only when the fan physically moves air (T3), not during initial handshake or flap transit.
* **The Rule:** The duration timer (default $5\text{ minutes}$) begins after the flap reaches position and the fan completes startup boost. If equilibrium is not achieved when the timer expires, EQ halts purge and reports `TIME_LIMIT_REACHED`.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A 5-minute purge achieves equilibrium at minute 3. The fan stops immediately, reporting `EQUILIBRIUM_ACHIEVED`, saving 2 minutes of fan run-time.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A large room fails to equilibrate within 5 minutes. The timer expires at minute 5. EQ halts the fan and returns `TIME_LIMIT_REACHED`. The Thermostat receives the terminal status and takes over conditioning using mechanical stages.

### CMP-04: Hub-Loss Invalidation Rule
* **The Decision:** Because Compliance is a shared inter-node agreement, loss of Hub communication immediately terminates the purge cycle.
* **The Rule:** If a valid Hub ping is not received within the configured timeout during an active purge, Compliance terminates, the fan returns to $0\%$, and the mode reverts to the pre-compliance state.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Ping packets remain stable. Purge runs to completion with status reporting delivered back to the Thermostat.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Wi-Fi drops during a high-speed purge cycle. Without Hub supervision, the Thermostat might activate heating while the EQ continues venting. Hub-loss detection triggers, aborting Compliance and returning the EQ to its autonomous baseline.

---

# 10. Operational Matrix & Safety Interlock Subsystem

## 10.1 Architecture & ownership

The Operational Matrix (OM) is a **static, compile-time rulebook** executed by each subsystem prior to actuation (`GLOB-20`). It is not an active controller or task loop. 

* **Component-Local Matrices (`GLOB-36`):** The Fan and Flap evaluate separate matrices. One component's OM cannot directly call another component's OM.
* **Conjunctive AND Evaluation (`GLOB-37`):** All required conditions combine as declarative equations.
* **Universal Severity Order (`GLOB-39`):**
  $$\text{PERMITTED} < \text{DEGRADED} < \text{UNAVAILABLE} < \text{PROHIBITED}$$

## 10.2 Operational Matrix decisions & operational rules

### OM-01: Declarative AND Gating
* **The Decision:** Operations are evaluated against declarative Boolean equations across current system state.
* **The Rule:** All required predicates must evaluate to true for an operation to be permitted:
  $$\text{Operation Permitted} = \text{Safety Clear} \land \text{No Lockout} \land \text{Interlocks Satisfied} \land \text{Resources Valid}$$
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Fan start requested. E-stop is clear, lockout is off, flap is open, and sensors are valid. Predicates evaluate to true; OM returns `PERMITTED`.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** All operational parameters are valid, but a maintenance lockout flag is set. The predicate chain evaluates to false; OM returns `PROHIBITED`.

### OM-02: Universal Severity Resolution
* **The Decision:** When multiple conditions evaluate simultaneously, the OM returns the single most restrictive outcome based on the severity hierarchy.
* **The Rule:** Any condition resulting in `PROHIBITED` overrides all `DEGRADED` or `UNAVAILABLE` states.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Primary sensor is missing (`DEGRADED`), but an approved fallback sensor is available. No safety rules are violated. OM returns `DEGRADED`, allowing open-loop operation.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An optional display is disconnected (`DEGRADED`), and physical emergency stop triggers (`PROHIBITED`). The severity engine resolves to `PROHIBITED`, suppressing actuation while logging both conditions.

### OM-03: Maintenance Lockout Scope
* **The Decision:** Maintenance Lockout inhibits all active control target generation (Auto, Manual, Compliance) and blocks physical actuation at the driver boundary, while keeping sensor sampling active.
* **The Rule:** When active:
  1. Auto, Manual, and Compliance control loops are suspended from generating output targets.
  2. Physical Fan Driver rejects all actuation requests.
  3. Sensors continue updating; diagnostics remain active.
  4. Lockout state persists across reboots in NVS.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A technician engages Maintenance Lockout. The fan stops immediately. The technician views live temperature and RPM telemetry on the service screen while servicing the physical unit safely.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A unit in Lockout experiences an unexpected power loss. Upon reboot, the system re-reads the persisted lockout flag from NVS. Control loops remain suspended, preventing unexpected motor startup during ongoing maintenance.

### OM-04: Emergency Stop Driver-Level Inhabitation
* **The Decision:** Physical emergency stop acts directly on actuator drivers via the Operational Matrix.
* **The Rule:** When active:
  * Fan Driver de-energizes the power relay and sets PWM to $0\%$.
  * Flap Driver detaches servo power at its current position (no open or close motion).
  * Actuation requests are rejected until E-stop is released and an explicit re-evaluation cycle runs.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Normal operation. E-stop line is high (healthy). Actuator drivers process incoming commands smoothly.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Mechanical duct failure occurs; operator hits physical E-stop. The E-stop input goes low. The Fan relay de-energizes, and the Flap servo drops PWM hold. The flap does not close against spinning blades, avoiding physical damage.

---

# 11. Fault Engine & Health Subsystem

## 11.1 The three-dimensional fault model

The Fault Engine decouples physical observations from persistent safety restrictions across three dimensions (`GLOB-26`):

```text
1. OBSERVED HEALTH      Current live sensor/feedback line reading.
2. ACTIVE FAULT         Classified operational failure meeting debounce thresholds.
3. RETAINED LATCH       Persistent restriction stored in NVS; requires manual clearance.
```

## 11.2 Fault engine decisions & operational rules

### FHT-01: Separation of Observation, Fault, and Latch
* **The Decision:** A healthy physical reading does not clear an active latch. Latching policy is defined per fault type.
* **The Rule:** Recovery evaluation:
  * Transient faults auto-clear after a configured number of consecutive valid evaluation cycles.
  * Latching faults remain active in memory and flash until a validated `Clear-Fault` command is executed.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A tachometer wire suffers transient electrical noise. The signal normalizes, passes three consecutive evaluation windows, and the active fault flag clears automatically.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Severe motor over-current occurs, tripping a latching `FAN_OVERCURRENT` fault. Power is cut. A technician checks the unit and finds the motor free. Fresh sensor readings report normal resistance, but the latch remains active in NVS, preventing motor operation until the technician enters an explicit clear command.

### FHT-02: Reboot Reconciliation with Flash Latches
* **The Decision:** On boot, fresh POST evaluations reconcile with persisted flash records.
* **The Rule:** Reconciliation logic:
  * Persisted ordinary fault + healthy POST $\implies$ Clear fault.
  * Persisted ordinary fault + failing POST $\implies$ Keep fault active.
  * Persisted Latch + healthy POST $\implies$ **Keep Latch Active**.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A temporary communication failure occurred prior to reboot. On boot, POST verifies the communication link is healthy. The ordinary fault is cleared, and the device enters `READY` mode.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An operator reboots a locked-out unit to try to bypass a safety latch. On boot, POST checks pass, but the startup routine reads the active latch from flash, blocks initialization, and returns the device to `SAFE_MODE`.

### FHT-03: Clear-Fault Is an Override, Not Proof of Repair
* **The Decision:** Executing a `Clear-Fault` command resets the recorded latch state to allow re-evaluation, but does not certify hardware health.
* **The Rule:** Clearing a latch allows the Operational Matrix to re-evaluate operating permissions. If physical fault conditions persist, the Fault Engine re-latches the fault according to configured thresholds.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A technician clears a latch after replacing a damaged fan belt. The Fault Engine clears the latch, the OM permits operation, and the fan resumes normal runtime.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An operator executes `Clear-Fault` on a fan with a locked rotor. The latch clears. The driver attempts a low-speed start, detects stall conditions over three consecutive cycles, and re-engages the safety latch, shutting down the unit.

---

# 12. Mode Transition Scheduler

## 12.1 Scheduling architecture

The Scheduler manages time-delayed, recurring, and duration-based transitions. It acts as a **semantic command requester**, operating through the standard Command Manager rather than directly controlling actuators (`GLOB-21`).

```text
Schedule Container (NVS Persisted or RAM Only)
          ↓
Tasks (Delayed, Recurring, Duration-Based)
          ↓
Execution Evaluator (Monotonic time for durations; Wall-clock for calendar)
          ↓
Semantic Request Emitted to Command Manager (e.g., SET_MODE(AUTO))
          ↓
Standard Authority & Safety Pipeline Evaluates Request
```

## 12.2 Scheduler decisions & operational rules

### SCH-01: Request Generator Boundary
* **The Decision:** The Scheduler submits semantic command requests; it has zero direct access to physical actuator drivers.
* **The Rule:** When a scheduled task fires, it builds a command packet and submits it to the Command Manager. The request is evaluated against current safety and operational matrix rules like any external command.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A calendar task fires at 22:00 requesting `OFF`. The command routes through the Command Manager, updates the mode, and executes a controlled shutdown.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A task fires at 08:00 requesting `MANUAL` with $80\%$ fan speed while an emergency stop is active. The request is submitted, rejected by the Operational Matrix, and marked as `BLOCKED [REASON: EMERGENCY_STOP]`. The scheduler records the failure without bypassing safety.

### SCH-02: Timing Reference Separation
* **The Decision:** Duration-based tasks use monotonic system time; calendar tasks use UTC wall-clock time.
* **The Rule:** Time reference selection:
  * Relative delays (e.g., "run for 45 minutes") track non-decreasing monotonic ticks.
  * Time-of-day tasks (e.g., "switch to ECO at 20:00") track validated real-time clock references.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A 2-hour manual override timer runs. An NTP sync adjusts the local wall clock backward by 3 minutes. The monotonic timer ignores the shift and expires after exactly 120 elapsed minutes.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A calendar task is set for 06:00 UTC. The node loses Wi-Fi, and the RTC loses synchronization. The scheduler marks clock validity as `UNTRUSTED`, suspends calendar tasks, logs a diagnostic warning, and keeps local monotonic timers running.

### SCH-03: Explicit Conflict Resolution
* **The Decision:** Conflicting scheduled tasks are identified at configuration time and will not silently overwrite active tasks.
* **The Rule:** Installing a new task that overlaps an existing task's execution window requires an explicit update or replacement flag.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An operator schedules a daily purge cycle. The validator confirms no conflicting tasks exist, and writes the schedule to flash.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An automation tries to install a 3-hour manual run that overlaps an existing compliance window. The scheduler rejects the write with `SCHEDULE_CONFLICT_DETECTED`, preserving the existing task schedule.

---

# 13. Canonical Command & Event Pipeline

## 13.1 Universal inbound command pathway

Every command source converges onto an identical pipeline (`GLOB-10`):

```text
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   EXTERNAL COMMAND INGESTION SOURCES                   │
│   Web Server REST │ Modbus Registers │ BLE GATT │ Hub Packets │ UI Dial│
└───────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┘
                                    │
                                    ▼
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ 1. Deserialization & Protocol Adapter (Maps to Canonical Struct)       │
│ 2. Parameter Validation (Range checks, format verification)            │
│ 3. Operational Matrix Gating (Safety interlocks, permission check)     │
│ 4. Subsystem Dispatch (Delivered to authoritative domain owner)        │
│ 5. State Machine Commit (Internal state updated atomically)            │
│ 6. Outcome Generation (REJECTED, BLOCKED, APPLIED, FAILED)             │
│ 7. Event Bus Emission (Published to subscribers post-commit)           │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

## 13.2 Pipeline decisions & operational rules

### CEP-01: Commit Precedes Event Emission
* **The Decision:** State-change events are emitted only after the internal state has been updated in memory.
* **The Rule:** No event bus notification may be dispatched while a state change is pending or unverified.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A setpoint update is processed. The Configuration Core validates and applies the new value, then emits `SETPOINT_CHANGED`. Subscribers receive the event and read the updated value immediately.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A setpoint change encounters a memory allocation error during application. The transaction aborts. Because events fire only post-commit, no event is emitted, preventing downstream services from acting on a phantom change.

### CEP-02: Physical User Actions Enter via the Command Manager
* **The Decision:** Local controls (buttons, rotary encoders) issue semantic commands to the Command Manager rather than injecting values into the Resource Engine (`GLOB-41`).
* **The Rule:** Turning a knob generates a `REQUEST_SETPOINT` command. The command passes through range validation, safety gating, and authority checks before any setting changes.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An operator turns a dial. The UI adapter sends a command to increase fan speed. The command is validated, approved by the OM, and applied through the Fan Driver.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A child rapidly spins a dial beyond maximum limits while the unit is locked out. The raw inputs generate out-of-range commands that are rejected at the validation gate, protecting the control loop from erratic behavior.

---

# 14. Configuration & Flash Persistence Core

## 14.1 Transactional persistence pipeline

To protect flash memory on ESP32 hardware, configuration changes follow an atomic pipeline (`GLOB-14`):

```text
Request Change → Validate Transaction → Apply In-Memory → Commit to NVS → Publish Event
```

## 14.2 Persistence decisions & operational rules

### CFG-01: Flash Write Isolation & Debounce Coalescing
* **The Decision:** Routine operational state, dynamic control targets, and live sensor readings are never written to flash.
* **The Rule:** Only validated configuration changes (setpoints, calibration tables, network parameters) are written to NVS. Rapid adjustments (e.g., slider inputs) are debounced in RAM and committed in a single atomic write after input settles.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A user drags a temperature slider through 20 intermediate steps before stopping at $22^\circ\text{C}$. The RAM target updates dynamically while the NVS write debounces for $3\text{ seconds}$, writing to flash only once.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A buggy control loop recalculates fan targets every $500\text{ ms}$. Because control targets are categorized as volatile runtime state, zero flash writes occur, protecting the ESP32 flash memory from premature wear.

### CFG-02: Recovery from Corrupted Stored Configuration
* **The Decision:** Corrupted NVS configuration triggers fallback to verified factory defaults with diagnostic reporting.
* **The Rule:** All configuration structures carry CRC32 integrity checksums. If a checksum fails at boot, the driver rejects the corrupted block, loads hard-coded factory defaults, logs a `NVS_CRC_CORRUPT` diagnostic, and flags the system for review.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** On boot, configuration blocks pass CRC checks, load into memory, and normal startup proceeds.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An unexpected power cut during a legacy write corrupts flash memory. On the next boot, the CRC check fails. Instead of running with corrupted parameters, the unit loads safe factory defaults ($24^\circ\text{C}$ Auto mode), logs the incident, and remains operational.

---

# 15. Node Lifecycle & Firmware-Managed Safe Mode

## 15.1 Boot sequence & dependency evaluation

```text
┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐
│  BOOT / HAL  │  →  │ CONFIG LOAD  │  →  │ POST CHECKS  │  →  │ RECONCILE    │
│ Platform Init│     │ Verify CRC32 │     │ Fresh Sensor │     │ NVS Latches  │
└──────────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────┬───────┘
                                                                      │
                         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┴────────┐
                         ▼                                                     ▼
               All Critical Checks Pass                           Critical Dependency Fails
                         │                                                     │
                         ▼                                                     ▼
         ┌───────────────────────────────┐                     ┌───────────────────────────────┐
         │     READY / NORMAL MODE       │                     │          SAFE MODE            │
         │ Full local control authorized │                     │ Actuators disabled & detached │
         │  Degraded if optional missing │                     │ Comms & Diagnostics active    │
         └───────────────────────────────┘                     └───────────────────────────────┘
```

## 15.2 Lifecycle decisions & operational rules

### LIF-01: Firmware-Managed Safe Mode Isolation
* **The Decision:** Failure of a critical hardware or software dependency during POST transitions the node into a firmware-managed Safe Mode rather than entering an uncontained reboot loop.
* **The Rule:** In Safe Mode:
  1. Actuator drivers are halted and detached.
  2. Climate control loops are suspended.
  3. Network interfaces, web diagnostics (`/status`), and logging remain active.
  4. The node accepts diagnostic and configuration recovery commands.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** POST verifies all peripherals, sensor buses, and calibration tables. Node enters `READY` mode and begins climate tracking.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An I2C bus fails during startup, taking down the primary safety sensors. Instead of panic-looping the ESP32, the node enters `SAFE_MODE`. Physical actuators remain safely unpowered, and the web diagnostic interface stays online to report the failed bus to technicians.

### LIF-02: Progressive Recovery with Bounded Retries
* **The Decision:** Safe Mode implements scheduled, bounded dependency re-tests to recover automatically from transient hardware faults.
* **The Rule:** The node re-tests failed dependencies using exponential backoff. If checks pass and no latches are set, the node transitions to `DEGRADED` or `READY`. Retry attempts are bounded to prevent bus flooding.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An external sensor module experiences delayed power-up. POST fails on attempt 1, placing the node in Safe Mode. The 10-second re-test detects the initialized sensor, passes validation, and promotes the node to `READY`.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A sensor is permanently damaged. The progressive recovery engine retries at 10s, 30s, 60s, and then caps at 5-minute intervals. The node remains in Safe Mode without saturating the CPU or spamming network logs.

---

# 16. Communication Adapters & Cross-Node Contracts

## 16.1 Transport independence

Protocol adapters (Modbus, BLE, HTTP, MQTT, CAN) act as transport translators. They map external frames to canonical internal command structs and serialize internal state snapshots for external delivery (`GLOB-01`). Adapters contain zero business logic.

## 16.2 Cross-node contracts & operational rules

### COM-01: On-Demand Sensor Queries (`REP-013`)
* **The Decision:** Every node can serve individual sensor readings to any requesting peer on demand.
* **The Rule:** The response includes: value, canonical unit, quality tag, and measurement timestamp.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** The Thermostat queries the EQ for outdoor temperature. The EQ responds with $295.15\text{ K}$, quality `GOOD`, timestamped $1.2\text{ seconds}$ ago. The Thermostat uses the reading directly.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A query arrives while the outdoor sensor is disconnected. The EQ responds with value $0$, quality `UNAVAILABLE`, and an error code. The Thermostat receives the payload and falls back to an alternate provider, avoiding bad data.

### COM-02: Complete Node State Snapshots (`REP-014`)
* **The Decision:** Nodes provide a complete, serialized state snapshot on demand.
* **The Rule:** Snapshots bundle node identity, lifecycle state, active configuration, resource qualities, actuator states, active faults, and latches. Reconnecting peers query this snapshot before subscribing to incremental updates.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A mobile dashboard connects to the local network. It requests `/snapshot` via HTTP, populates all UI screens from the single response, and begins listening for incremental change events.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A temporary network drop causes a monitoring node to miss several change events. Upon reconnecting, the node requests a fresh snapshot, reconciling its internal state with current truth and discarding out-of-order packets.

---

# 17. Observability, Live Inspection & Local UI

## 17.1 Observability architecture

EQ provides visibility through three distinct channels:
1. **Console Debug Logging:** Filtered serial output for firmware debugging.
2. **Dedicated `/status` Inspection:** A read-only web endpoint providing comprehensive diagnostic telemetry.
3. **Local OLED / Dial UI:** Hardware display showing operational summaries and supporting basic setpoint adjustments.

## 17.2 Observability decisions & operational rules

### OBS-01: Dedicated Read-Only `/status` Endpoint
* **The Decision:** Live runtime inspection is exposed via a dedicated read-only web endpoint (`/status`), completely separate from control interfaces.
* **The Rule:** The `/status` page has zero command inputs, buttons, or mutation endpoints. It provides observability without introducing a back-door control path.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A field technician connects to the device's IP and opens `/status`. The page displays live sensor readings, ramp targets, loop errors, and active OM permissions without requiring authentication or risking accidental setting changes.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A penetration tester attempts to inject a POST request with new setpoint parameters into the `/status` route. The web server rejects the request immediately; the endpoint only implements read-only GET methods.

### OBS-02: Local UI as an Untrusted Adapter
* **The Decision:** The local OLED display and rotary dial operate as an interface adapter, not a business logic owner.
* **The Rule:** The OLED reads state via read-only APIs. The rotary dial submits commands to the Command Manager. The display subsystem owns no internal control variables.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A user turns the dial to change the temperature. The screen shows `Pending...`, the command is validated, applied, and the screen updates with confirmation.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** The OLED controller suffers an I2C lockup while the display is updating. The core climate loops continue running on their independent FreeRTOS task, and the fan maintains its target without interruption.

---

# 18. Performance Constraints & Layered Testing

## 18.1 Embedded resource budgets (ESP32)

* **Bounded Loop Execution:** Control loop evaluations must complete within a deterministic $20\text{ ms}$ execution budget.
* **Zero Dynamic Allocation in Core Loops:** Processing cycles must not allocate heap memory (`malloc`, `new`). Static structures, buffers, and ring buffers are allocated at initialization.
* **Separation of Cores:** Time-critical actuator PWM generation, tachometer counting, and safety interlocks run on FreeRTOS Core 0. High-level networking, web serving, and display rendering run on Core 1.

## 18.2 Three-tier testing requirements

```text
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                        3-TIER VERIFICATION MODEL                       │
├──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────────────────┤
│ Tier 1: Unit Tests       │ Tier 2: Integration     │ Tier 3: Hardware  │
│ Host-based (Native/POSIX)│ Multi-subsystem testing │ Physical ESP32    │
│ Algorithmic boundaries,  │ Resource Engine to OM,  │ GPIO, PWM, Relay, │
│ Kelvin error math, ramp  │ Command to Driver,      │ Bus timeouts, I2C │
│ stepping profiles.       │ POST reconciliation.    │ noise, power cuts.│
└──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────────────┘
```

1. **Tier 1 (Host Unit Tests):** Verifies state machine transitions, math precision, and validation routines in continuous integration.
2. **Tier 2 (Integration Tests):** Verifies multi-subsystem pipelines (e.g., verifying that a sensor timeout correctly triggers the 5-minute fallback countdown before transferring authority to Manual).
3. **Tier 3 (Physical Target Tests):** Hardware verification on real ESP32 silicon under physical fault conditions (relay arcing, power dips, I2C bus disconnections).

---

# 19. Canonical EQ Decision Register

The canonical decision register catalogs all locked decisions across the EQ architecture. Entries are permanent, append-only, and retain their historical identifiers.

```text
                                 EQ DECISION REGISTER INDEX
┌──────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬────────────┐
│ CORE-001..26 │ Operating Modes, Node Identity, and Baseline Autonomy   │ LOCKED     │
│ FAN-001..095 │ Fan PWM Mapping, Startup, Feedback, and Calibration     │ LOCKED     │
│ FLAP-001..91 │ Flap Angles, Monotonic Curves, Settling, and Detaching  │ LOCKED     │
│ FANFLAP-1..6 │ Fan ↔ Flap Safety Invariants and Minimum Curve Points   │ LOCKED     │
│ AUTO-001..78 │ Kelvin Math, Equilibrium Gating, and Floor Scaling      │ LOCKED     │
│ COMP-001..58 │ Thermostat Coordination, Purge Limits, and Handshakes   │ LOCKED     │
│ MCA-A01..A20 │ Mode Transitions and Deterministic Precedence Hierarchy │ LOCKED     │
│ SCHED-01..32 │ Monotonic Timers, Task Scheduling, and Non-Preemption   │ LOCKED     │
│ FHR-001..014 │ Health vs. Fault vs. Latch and Safe Recovery Clearances │ LOCKED     │
│ SAF-001..012 │ Emergency Stop Execution, Lockouts, and OM Safety Gating│ LOCKED     │
│ CE-001..014  │ Canonical Command Pipeline and Post-Commit Events       │ LOCKED     │
│ CFG-001..012 │ Transactional Flash, Debouncing, and Reset Scopes       │ LOCKED     │
│ COM-001..012 │ Transport Independence and Adapter Layering             │ LOCKED     │
│ REP-001..018 │ Cross-Node Queries, Snapshots, and Recency Filtering    │ LOCKED     │
│ LIFE-001..13 │ POST Sequencing, Boot Reconciliations, and Boundaries   │ LOCKED     │
│ SAFE-001..10 │ Firmware-Managed Safe Mode and Isolated Actuation       │ LOCKED     │
│ DIA-001..014 │ Structured Diagnostics and Reason Codes                 │ LOCKED     │
│ LIVE-001..17 │ Dedicated Read-Only `/status` and Real-Time Inspection  │ LOCKED     │
│ UI-001..012  │ Local OLED Display and Rotary Encoder Adapter Contracts │ LOCKED     │
│ TEST-001..12 │ 3-Tier Testing Suite and Negative Verification Rules    │ LOCKED     │
└──────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴────────────┘
```

---

### Core node architecture (`CORE-001` – `CORE-026`)
* **`CORE-001` (Operating Modes):** Device implements `OFF`, `AUTO/TEMPERATURE`, `AUTO/HUMIDITY`, `MANUAL`, and `COMPLIANCE`.
* **`CORE-002` (Fault Runtime State):** Fault is an operational health condition, not an operating mode.
* **`CORE-003` (Any-to-Any Transitions):** Operating modes transition flexibly, subject to validation and safety gating.
* **`CORE-004` (Single Command Gateway):** All mode transitions pass through the unified command pipeline.
* **`CORE-005` (Implicit Auto Temperature):** Updating temperature setpoints implicitly selects `AUTO/TEMPERATURE`.
* **`CORE-006` (Implicit Auto Humidity):** Updating humidity setpoints implicitly selects `AUTO/HUMIDITY`.
* **`CORE-007` (Per-Domain Authority):** Actuator domains (Fan, Flap) resolve authority independently.
* **`CORE-008` (Manual Direct Transfer):** Direct user actuator commands set the respective domain to `MANUAL`.
* **`CORE-009` (No Redundant Selectors):** Device UI avoids separate submode toggles, relying on setpoint selection.
* **`CORE-010` (Safety Dominance):** Safety interlocks hold highest authority over all normal control modes.
* **`CORE-011` (Active Off State):** Mode `OFF` disables climate outputs while keeping monitoring, safety, and communications active.
* **`CORE-012` (Shutdown Sequencing):** Mode `OFF` cuts fan power before fully closing the vent flap.
* **`CORE-013` (Offline Autonomy):** Loss of Hub communication does not disrupt ongoing local regulation.
* **`CORE-014` (Target vs. Actual):** Commanded targets are strictly decoupled from physical feedback state.
* **`CORE-015` (Authoritative State Model):** Node maintains one centralized state model; interfaces do not reconstruct state.
* **`CORE-016` (Dual Atomic Changes):** Commands can modify configuration parameters and runtime modes within a single transaction.
* **`CORE-017` (Normalized Quality Tags):** Every resource measurement carries an explicit quality tag.
* **`CORE-018` (Quality State Enum):** System recognizes `GOOD`, `STALE`, `INVALID`, `TIMEOUT`, `DISCONNECTED`, `OUT_OF_RANGE`, and `UNAVAILABLE`.
* **`CORE-019` (Atomic Provider Switching):** Changing a sensor provider validates live readings before transferring authority.
* **`CORE-020` (Logical Resource Scope):** Node provisions internal/external temperature, humidity, fan feedback, flap position, and monotonic time.
* **`CORE-021` (Monotonic Durations):** All durations, timeouts, and ramp intervals use non-decreasing monotonic time.
* **`CORE-022` (Wall-Clock Calendar):** Wall-clock time is reserved for human calendar schedules and logging.
* **`CORE-023` (No False Feedback):** Software never fabricates measured positions when physical feedback sensors are absent.
* **`CORE-024` (Domain Separation):** Fan and Flap domains interact via declarative interlocks, never direct driver writes.
* **`CORE-025` (Fan/Flap Invariant):** Running the fan requires the flap to satisfy its minimum safe opening.
* **`CORE-026` (Proportional Flap Openings):** The flap opens proportionally to fan speed based on the active curve, rather than binary switching.

---

### Fan control subsystem (`FAN-001` – `FAN-095`)
* **`FAN-001` (Flap Capability Bypass):** If flap hardware is configured as disabled, the flap interlock evaluates as satisfied.
* **`FAN-002` (0–100% Target Scale):** Fan algorithms reason in normalized $0\text{--}100\%$ targets regardless of physical PWM bounds.
* **`FAN-003` (Zero Cutoff via Relay):** Commanded $0\%$ triggers an orderly ramp-down followed by relay isolation.
* **`FAN-004` (Linear PWM Mapping):** Logical $1\text{--}100\%$ scales linearly across physical minimum and maximum PWM bounds.
* **`FAN-005` (Persisted PWM Bounds):** Physical PWM limits are stored in NVS, read at boot, and require reboot to change.
* **`FAN-006` (Decoupled Run Requests):** Fan Target speed and Fan Run Enable requests are managed as separate parameters.
* **`FAN-007` (Unified Mode Target Path):** Manual, Auto, and Compliance targets feed into an identical ramp and safety pipeline.
* **`FAN-008` (Slew Rate Profiling):** Target changes follow a unified, configurable slew rate profiler.
* **`FAN-009` (Target Preemption):** Incoming target updates supersede in-flight ramps, steering toward the newest target.
* **`FAN-010` (Interlock Sequencing):** Startup requires confirming the flap opening threshold before closing the relay.
* **`FAN-011` (Relay Settling Delay):** The driver pauses $500\text{ ms}$ after closing the relay before applying PWM.
* **`FAN-012` (Low-Speed Boost):** Starts below $20\%$ apply a $20\%$ PWM boost for $2.0\text{ seconds}$ to break static friction.
* **`FAN-013` (Startup Verification):** Post-boost validation verifies tachometer feedback before confirming normal running status.
* **`FAN-014` (Tachless Startup Assumptions):** Units without tachometers assume successful startup based on elapsed timer completion.
* **`FAN-015` (Latching Startup Failures):** Repeated failed startup attempts latch a structured `FAN_STARTUP_FAULT`.
* **`FAN-016` (Orderly Shutdown Path):** Shutdown ramps PWM to $0\%$, confirms stop, pauses $10\text{ seconds}$, then opens the relay.
* **`FAN-017` (Power-Down Dissipation):** Power relay remains energized for $10\text{ seconds}$ after PWM reaches zero.
* **`FAN-018` (Restart Monotonic Lockout):** Minimum restart lockout timing begins from the moment the relay opens.
* **`FAN-019` (Shutdown Interruption):** Re-commanding run during the $10\text{-second}$ power-down window reuses the active relay path smoothly.
* **`FAN-020` (Safety Stop Pathway):** Emergency stops route through the driver pipeline, ramping down and cutting relay power.
* **`FAN-021` (Feedback vs. Control):** Tachometers provide passive feedback; they do not directly dictate fan speed.
* **`FAN-022` (Dual Feedback Isolation):** Tachometer RPM and Current Sensing operate as distinct, isolated diagnostic resources.
* **`FAN-023` (Decoupled Filtering):** Raw sensor sampling is separated from operational feedback evaluation logic.
* **`FAN-024` (No Arbitrary Stall Limits):** Stall detection is calibrated to actual hardware capabilities, avoiding arbitrary minimums.
* **`FAN-025` (Triple Window Filter):** Validation failure requires three consecutive failed evaluation windows.
* **`FAN-026` (Sensor Disagreement Flag):** Discrepancies between current draw and tachometer rotation raise dedicated diagnostic flags.
* **`FAN-027` (Tachless Mode Configuration):** Hardware units without tachometer lines explicitly disable tachometer validation routines.
* **`FAN-028` (Time-Based Open-Loop Mode):** Tachless operation relies on open-loop voltage scaling and time-based ramp profiling.
* **`FAN-029` (Controlled Degraded State):** Degraded operation is permitted only when authorized by the Operational Matrix.
* **`FAN-030` (Decoupled Health vs. Config):** Sensor read failures mark runtime resources unavailable without disabling the hardware capability flag.
* **`FAN-031` (Service Calibration Authority):** Calibration commands run as explicit service operations with temporary Fan authority.
* **`FAN-032` (Atomic Calibration Commit):** Calibration sequences commit to flash only after all points pass validation checks.
* **`FAN-033` (Calibration Sensor Dependency):** Calibration requires at least one functioning feedback sensor (RPM or Current).
* **`FAN-034` (Deterministic Step Tables):** Calibration steps through deterministic intervals ($0, N, 2N, \dots, 100\%$).
* **`FAN-035` (Triple Calibration Retries):** Unstable calibration points permit up to three retries before failing the sequence.
* **`FAN-036` (Quick vs. Strict Profiles):** System supports both thorough Strict calibration and expedited Quick calibration routines.
* **`FAN-037` (Calibration Invalidation):** Changing physical PWM limits invalidates existing calibration tables.
* **`FAN-038` (Persisted Maintenance Lockout):** Maintenance lockout blocks motor operation, survives reboots, and preserves target intent in RAM.
* **`FAN-039` (Clear-Fault Start Invariant):** Executing `Clear-Fault` resets fault latches without directly starting the motor.
* **`FAN-040` (Pre-Clearance Verification):** Fault clearance requires verifying the physical fault condition has cleared.
* **`FAN-041`–`095` (Core Driver Rules):** Detailed PWM timing, settling windows, slew rates, feedback validation, and diagnostic contracts.

---

### Flap control subsystem (`FLAP-001` – `FLAP-091`)
* **`FLAP-001` (Logical Position Scale):** Flap positions reason in normalized $0\text{--}100\%$ ($0\% = \text{closed}$, $100\% = \text{open}$).
* **`FLAP-002` (Physical Endpoint Mapping):** Physical servo pulse width limits ($1.0\text{--}2.0\text{ ms}$) are configured independently.
* **`FLAP-003` (Directional Inversion):** Linkage inversion flips physical pulse mappings while preserving logical $0\text{--}100\%$ meaning.
* **`FLAP-004` (Logical Deadband Tolerance):** Movements within configured tolerance ($1\%$) do not trigger servo adjustments.
* **`FLAP-005` (Configurable Fan Curve):** Proportional flap movement maps from fan speed via a $2\text{--}20$ point curve.
* **`FLAP-006` (Bounded Curve Points):** Curves require at least 2 points (endpoints) and support up to 20 points.
* **`FLAP-007` (Linear Segment Math):** Values between defined curve points are linearly interpolated.
* **`FLAP-008` (Monotonic Non-Decreasing):** Curves must be monotonic non-decreasing; higher fan speeds cannot command smaller flap openings.
* **`FLAP-009` (Integer Fan Percentages):** Curve points use integer fan percentages ($0\text{--}100\%$).
* **`FLAP-010` (Atomic Curve Commits):** New curves are validated as a complete set; invalid curves are rejected without modifying flash.
* **`FLAP-011` (Point 1 Safety Foundation):** Point 1 establishes minimum running fan speed and required opening threshold.
* **`FLAP-012` (Upper Bound Clamping):** Fan speeds exceeding the final curve point clamp to the final flap target.
* **`FLAP-013` (Supported Operating Modes):** Subsystem supports `PROPORTIONAL_CURVE` and `FULL_OPEN_FULL_CLOSED`.
* **`FLAP-014` (Binary Mode Operation):** In full-open/full-closed mode, the flap opens fully on fan start and closes on fan stop.
* **`FLAP-015` (Curve Strategy Separation):** The curve acts as a target-generation strategy, not an independent control authority.
* **`FLAP-016` (Single Effective Target):** Conflicting flap requests resolve to a single target before reaching the driver.
* **`FLAP-017` (Flap Authority Hierarchy):** Authority follows fixed precedence: $\text{Safety} > \text{POST} > \text{Fan Running} > \text{Manual}$.
* **`FLAP-018` (Authority vs. Movement):** Control authority (who commands) is distinct from physical movement state (where it is).
* **`FLAP-019` (Manual Static Positioning):** Users can position the flap manually when the fan is stopped.
* **`FLAP-020` (Running Manual Restrictions):** Manual flap commands are rejected while the fan is running, except for minimum close takeover.
* **`FLAP-021` (Manual Close Takeover):** Commanding $0\%$ flap while the fan runs initiates an orderly fan shutdown before closing.
* **`FLAP-022` (Volatile Takeover State):** Saved operating modes during manual takeover are kept in volatile RAM, not flash.
* **`FLAP-023` (Dynamic Takeover Tracking):** Mode changes during a takeover update the saved recovery target.
* **`FLAP-024` (Fan Preemption Over Flap):** A valid fan start command preempts manual flap positioning.
* **`FLAP-025` (Closure Blocked on Fan Stop):** Flap closure below the safety threshold waits until the fan confirms physical stop.
* **`FLAP-026` (Default Slew Timing):** Servo stepping defaults to $1\%$ increments every $50\text{ ms}$.
* **`FLAP-027` (In-Flight Preemption):** New flap targets supersede in-flight adjustments immediately.
* **`FLAP-028` (Stationary Power Savings):** The driver detaches servo PWM when position is within tolerance.
* **`FLAP-029` (Timed Post-Travel Settling):** Reaching target enters a $5.0\text{-second}$ settling window before detaching PWM.
* **`FLAP-030` (Interrupted Settling):** New targets received during settling cancel the delay and resume stepping.
* **`FLAP-031` (Unpowered Detached Coils):** Stationary flaps remain detached to prevent servo motor hum and wear.
* **`FLAP-032` (Discrete Movement States):** System reports: `CLOSED`, `OPENING`, `OPEN`, `CLOSING`, and `SETTLING`.
* **`FLAP-033` (No False Feedback Claims):** System reports commanded logical position, never fabricating measured physical feedback.
* **`FLAP-034` (No Phantom Travel Timeouts):** Missing position sensors preclude timing out on physical blade travel.
* **`FLAP-035` (No Phantom Actuator Faults):** System avoids declaring physical actuator faults without sensor feedback.
* **`FLAP-036` (Capability Disabling Isolation):** Disabling flap capability stops servo updates and POST sweeps.
* **`FLAP-037` (Config Retention on Disable):** Disabling flap hardware retains configuration parameters in flash.
* **`FLAP-038` (POST Travel Verification):** Flap POST sweeps min $\to$ max $\to$ min, verifying driver step completion.
* **`FLAP-039` (POST Driver Boundary):** POST requests motion through driver interfaces, never writing raw registers.
* **`FLAP-040` (Configurable POST Sweeps):** Flap POST sweeps can be disabled in configuration for silent deployments.
* **`FLAP-041`–`091` (Core Driver Rules):** Detailed pulse mapping, rate limiting, and driver lifecycle management.

---

### Auto climate & compliance subsystems (`AUTO-001` – `078`, `COMP-001` – `058`)
* **`AUTO-001`–`078` (Auto Climate Control):** Proportional environmental equilibrium tracking, floating-point Kelvin math, deadband management, target floor scaling, and sensor failure degradation.
* **`COMP-001`–`058` (Inter-Node Compliance):** Thermostat coordination protocol, heat/cool purge cycles, dynamic destination clamping, T3 monotonic runtime limits, and communication loss handling.

---

### Supporting platform engines (`MCA`, `SCHED`, `SAF`, `CE`, `CFG`, `LIFE`, `DIA`, `TEST`)
* **`MCA-A01`–`A20`:** Mode state machines, domain authority decoupling, and deterministic precedence.
* **`SCHED-A01`–`A32`:** Monotonic duration timers, calendar schedule validation, and command gateway dispatching.
* **`SAF-001`–`012`:** Operational Matrix gating, maintenance lockouts, and emergency-stop driver decoupling.
* **`CE-001`–`014`:** Canonical command pipelines, explicit outcome enums, and post-commit event dispatching.
* **`CFG-001`–`012`:** Transactional flash persistence, debounced writes, and CRC corruption fallbacks.
* **`LIFE-001`–`013` / `SAFE-001`–`010`:** Multi-stage POST sequencing, boot reconciliation, and firmware-managed Safe Mode.
* **`DIA-001`–`014` / `LIVE-001`–`017`:** Diagnostic formatting, read-only `/status` web serving, and live trace streams.
* **`TEST-001`–`012`:** Three-tier testing framework, negative testing requirements, and acceptance standards.

---
