display:
  - platform: ssd1306_i2c
    id: oled_display
    i2c_id: bus_internal
    model: "SSD1306 128x64"
    address: 0x3C
    update_interval: never
    lambda: |-
      if (!(id(vent_logic_mask) & 8)) {
        it.filled_rectangle(0, 0, it.get_width(), it.get_height(), COLOR_OFF);
        return; 
      }

      if (id(in_menu_mode)) {
        // ==========================
        // MENU RENDERING
        // ==========================
        it.filled_rectangle(0, 0, it.get_width(), it.get_height(), COLOR_OFF);
        
        if (id(menu_page) == 0) {
          it.printf(it.get_width() / 2, 0, id(small_font), TextAlign::TOP_CENTER, "MENU");
          it.line(10, 12, it.get_width() - 10, 12);
          const char* labels[5];
          if (id(operational_mode) == 3) labels[0] = "Mode: Comp";
          else if (id(operational_mode) == 1) labels[0] = "Mode: Temp";
          else if (id(operational_mode) == 2) labels[0] = "Mode: Hum";
          else labels[0] = "Mode: Manual";

          labels[1] = (id(operational_mode) == 2) ? "Target: Hum" : "Target: Temp";
          
          static char resp_buff[20];
          sprintf(resp_buff, "Resp: %s", id(response_mode_select).current_option().c_str());
          labels[2] = resp_buff;
          labels[3] = "Sensors >";
          labels[4] = id(fahrenheit_mode) ? "Unit: F" : "Unit: C";

          for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
            int y = 16 + (i * 9);
            if (i == id(menu_selection)) it.printf(4, y, id(small_font), TextAlign::TOP_LEFT, "> %s", labels[i]);
            else it.printf(12, y, id(small_font), TextAlign::TOP_LEFT, "%s", labels[i]);
          }
        }
        else if (id(menu_page) == 1) {
          it.printf(it.get_width() / 2, 0, id(small_font), TextAlign::TOP_CENTER, "SENSORS");
          it.line(10, 12, it.get_width() - 10, 12);
          
          const char* s_labels[2]; 
          static char src_buff[25];
          sprintf(src_buff, "Int: %s", id(internal_source_is_modbus) ? "Modbus" : "Wired");
          s_labels[0] = src_buff;
          s_labels[1] = "< Back";

          for (int i = 0; i < 2; i++) {
            int y = 16 + (i * 12);
            if (i == id(menu_selection)) it.printf(4, y, id(small_font), TextAlign::TOP_LEFT, "> %s", s_labels[i]);
            else it.printf(12, y, id(small_font), TextAlign::TOP_LEFT, "%s", s_labels[i]);
          }
        }
      } 
      else {
        // ==========================
        // DASHBOARD RENDERING
        // ==========================
        char buf[64];
        const char* unit = id(fahrenheit_mode) ? "F" : "C";
        auto to_d = [&](float c) -> float { return id(fahrenheit_mode) ? (c * 1.8f + 32.0f) : c; };

        // Row 1: Temperatures (Int/Ext)
        if (id(internal_temp_c).has_state() && id(external_temp_c).has_state()) {
          snprintf(buf, sizeof(buf), "%.1f/%.1f%s", to_d(id(internal_temp_c).state), to_d(id(external_temp_c).state), unit);
        } else { snprintf(buf, sizeof(buf), "--/--"); }
        it.printf(it.get_width() / 2, 0, id(big_font), TextAlign::TOP_CENTER, "%s", buf);

        // Row 2: Humidity (Int/Ext)
        if (id(internal_hum).has_state() && id(external_hum).has_state()) {
          snprintf(buf, sizeof(buf), "%.0f/%.0f%%", id(internal_hum).state, id(external_hum).state);
        } else { snprintf(buf, sizeof(buf), "--/--%%"); }
        it.printf(it.get_width() / 2, 18, id(big_font), TextAlign::TOP_CENTER, "%s", buf);

        // Row 3: Status Icons & Fault Alerts
        if (id(flap_is_open)) it.filled_rectangle(5, 38, 8, 8);
        else it.rectangle(5, 38, 8, 8);
        
        char m_buf[25];
        if (id(fan_stall_latched)) {
          snprintf(m_buf, sizeof(m_buf), "[STALL]");
        } else if (id(sensor_fault_active)) {
          snprintf(m_buf, sizeof(m_buf), "[FAULT]");
        } else if (id(fan_is_locked)) {
          snprintf(m_buf, sizeof(m_buf), "[LOCKED]");
        } else if (id(operational_mode) == 3) {
          snprintf(m_buf, sizeof(m_buf), "C:T %.1f%s", to_d(id(desired_temp_c)), unit);
        } else if (id(operational_mode) == 1) {
          snprintf(m_buf, sizeof(m_buf), "A:T %.1f%s", to_d(id(desired_temp_c)), unit);
        } else if (id(operational_mode) == 2) {
          snprintf(m_buf, sizeof(m_buf), "A:H %d%%", id(desired_hum));
        } else {
          snprintf(m_buf, sizeof(m_buf), "M %d%%", id(target_fan_speed_percent));
        }
        it.printf(18, 36, id(small_font), TextAlign::TOP_LEFT, "%s", m_buf);

        // Row 3 (Right): Fan Speed/RPM
        char rpm[8];
        if (!id(tach_enabled)) {
          snprintf(rpm, sizeof(rpm), "N/A");
        } else if (id(fan_rpm).has_state()) {
          snprintf(rpm, sizeof(rpm), "%.0f", id(fan_rpm).state);
        } else {
          snprintf(rpm, sizeof(rpm), "---");
        }
        snprintf(buf, sizeof(buf), "%d%%|%s", id(current_fan_speed_percent), rpm);
        it.printf(127, 36, id(small_font), TextAlign::TOP_RIGHT, "%s", buf);

        // Row 4: Min/Max Statistics
        if (id(show_min_stats)) {
          if (id(min_internal_temp_c) > -900.0) {
            snprintf(buf, sizeof(buf), "min: %.1f/%.1f%s|%.0f/%.0f%%", to_d(id(min_internal_temp_c)), to_d(id(min_external_temp_c)), unit, id(min_internal_hum), id(min_external_hum));
          } else { snprintf(buf, sizeof(buf), "min: --/-- | --/--"); }
        } else {
          if (id(max_internal_temp_c) > -900.0) {
            snprintf(buf, sizeof(buf), "max: %.1f/%.1f%s|%.0f/%.0f%%", to_d(id(max_internal_temp_c)), to_d(id(max_external_temp_c)), unit, id(max_internal_hum), id(max_external_hum));
          } else { snprintf(buf, sizeof(buf), "max: --/-- | --/--"); }
        }
        it.printf(it.get_width() / 2, 49, id(small_font), TextAlign::TOP_CENTER, "%s", buf);
      }

# Garuda Controls — Global Architectural Constitution, Node Frameworks & Decision Register

**Document Role:** Global Architectural Constitution and Ecosystem-Wide Standard  
**Governance Hierarchy:** Global Rules MD $\to$ Node-Specific Rules MD (EQ, Thermostat, Tanks) $\to$ Implementation (ESPHome YAML & Native C/C++)  
**Current Global Architecture Stage:** **Stage 0: COMPLETE** | **Stage 1: COMPLETE** | **Stage 2: READY TO START**  
**Ecosystem Scope:** Hub / Master, Equalizer (EQ), Thermostat (HVAC), Tanks Node, and future network participants  
**Implementation Target:** ESP32 family; ESPHome configuration base with custom native C/C++ (`.c`, `.cpp`, `.h`) core engines  

---

# 1. Master Index & Global Stage Gate Record

### Current Position: Global Stage 1 Formal Closure
Following the completion of the Equalizer (EQ) subsystem architecture, all ecosystem-wide frameworks, inter-node contracts, safety models, and data-quality engines are locked.

* **Stage 0 — Global Baseline & Reference Freeze:** **COMPLETE** (Legacy ecosystem behaviors cataloged and frozen as reference evidence).
* **Stage 1 — Global Architecture Definition:** **COMPLETE** (Universal engines, inter-node contracts, Operational Matrix model, and decision registers locked).
* **Stage 2 — Concrete Engine & Provider Implementation:** **READY TO START** (Active handoff point across all node types).

```text
┌───────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
│ GLOBAL ARCHITECTURE DOMAIN (STAGE 1)  │ CROSS-NODE IMPLEMENTATION HOME        │
├───────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
│ G1.1 Ownership & Semantic Boundaries  │ Stage 4: Central State & Enums        │
│ G1.2 Canonical Command Pipeline       │ Stage 5: FreeRTOS Command Deserializer│
│ G1.3 Physical Actuation Boundary      │ Stage 7: Hardware Drivers & HAL       │
│ G1.4 Universal Resource Engine        │ Stage 2: Provider Ingestion & Buffers │
│ G1.5 Node Typology & Autonomy         │ All Nodes: Identity & Config Core     │
│ G1.6 Safety, Authority & Fault Engine │ Stage 4 & 7: Fault Engine & Local OM  │
│ G1.7 Transactional Flash Persistence  │ Stage 3: Flash Wear & CRC Structs     │
│ G1.8 Scheduler & Timing Architecture  │ Stage 6: Monotonic & Calendar Queues  │
│ G1.9 State Publication & Snapshots    │ Stage 9 & 10: Serialization & REST    │
│ G1.10 Observability & Live Inspection │ Stage 10: Read-Only `/status` Web UI  │
│ G1.11 Standard Procedural Matrix      │ Engineering Governance & PR Gate      │
│ G1.12 Distributed Coordination Bus    │ Stage 9: CAN, BLE, ESP-NOW Adapters   │
│ G1.13 Declarative Operational Matrix  │ Stage 7: Local OM Code Tables         │
│ G1.14 Inter-Node Compliance Handshake │ Thermostat ↔ EQ Inter-Node Protocol   │
│ G1.15 Machine-Readable Reason Codes   │ Telemetry, UI & Error Code Headers    │
└───────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---

# 2. Document Authority, Hierarchy & Governance

## 2.1 Three-tier rule precedence

The ecosystem enforces a strict, non-negotiable rule hierarchy:

```text
GLOBAL ARCHITECTURAL RULES (This Document)
                  ↓
NODE-SPECIFIC ARCHITECTURAL RULES (EQ, Thermostat, Tanks, Hub)
                  ↓
IMPLEMENTATION SPECIFICATIONS (ESPHome YAML & Native C/C++)
```

1. **Global Rules (Tier 1):** Apply across all devices. If an abstraction, state machine, safety pattern, or data engine is needed by two or more node types (or defines how nodes communicate), it is defined globally.
2. **Node-Specific Rules (Tier 2):** Specialize global rules for a node's physical hardware, actuators, and dedicated control loops. Node rules may never silently contradict or weaken a global rule.
3. **Implementation Specifications (Tier 3):** Concrete code modules. Structural configurations and hardware pinouts reside in ESPHome YAML; control algorithms, state machines, and hardware drivers reside in C++.

## 2.2 Decision lifecycle & change control

```text
OPEN QUESTION → TECHNICAL EVALUATION → DECISION → ADR / REGISTER ENTRY → IMPLEMENTATION → VALIDATION
```

* **Zero Memory-Only Architecture:** No architectural principle may exist solely in conversation, code comments, or pull requests.
* **Non-Destructive Supersession:** Obsolete rules are never deleted. They are retained in the register, flagged as **SUPERSEDED**, and link to their replacement.
* **Stage Completion Law:** A stage is complete only when its explicit exit criteria are satisfied. Discussion movement does not imply completion.

---

# 3. Ecosystem Topology & Node Typology

The Garuda Controls ecosystem consists of four node types operating over a shared communication bus.

```text
                                 ┌─────────────────────────┐
                                 │       HUB / MASTER      │
                                 │ Coordination, Dashboard │
                                 │  WAN Gateway, Schedules │
                                 └────────────┬────────────┘
                                              │
                      Shared Communication Bus (Wi-Fi, BLE, ESP-NOW, Modbus, CAN)
                      ┌───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┐
                      ▼                       ▼                       ▼
            ┌──────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐
            │  EQUALIZER (EQ)  │    │ THERMOSTAT (AC)  │    │    TANKS NODE    │
            │ Climate Exchange │    │ HVAC Conditioning│    │ Fluid Monitoring │
            │   Fan & Flap     │    │ Reqs Compliance  │    │  Pumps & Valves  │
            └──────────────────┘    └──────────────────┘    └──────────────────┘
```

## 3.1 Node roles & responsibilities

### 1. Hub / Master
* **Role:** Ecosystem coordinator, data aggregator, WAN gateway, time provider, and central dashboard host.
* **Hardware Invariance:** Any device with sufficient compute, memory, and networking can serve as Master by running the core Hub coordination service.
* **Authority Boundary:** The Hub is **coordination infrastructure, not the control brain**. The Hub requests actions; individual nodes validate, authorize, and execute them locally.

### 2. Equalizer (EQ)
* **Role:** Autonomous indoor/outdoor climate balancer.
* **Hardware:** Ventilation fan, servo-driven vent flap, indoor/outdoor temperature and humidity probes.
* **Responsibilities:** Differential climate balancing, deadband management, physical fan/flap safety interlocks, and local purge routines.

### 3. Thermostat (HVAC)
* **Role:** Active climate conditioning node.
* **Hardware:** Heating/cooling relays, compressor stages, fan coils, indoor ambient sensors.
* **Inter-Node Role:** Operates in coordination with the EQ. Requests **Compliance Purge** from the EQ before or during active mechanical cycles to take advantage of favorable outdoor air.

### 4. Tanks Node
* **Role:** Fluid storage monitoring and liquid management.
* **Hardware:** Hydrostatic pressure transducers, ultrasonic level sensors, temperature probes, transfer pumps, and solenoid valves.
* **Responsibilities:** Volume integration, flow/consumption metrics, leak detection, and dry-run/overflow protection interlocks.

## 3.2 Universal node autonomy invariant

```text
HUB DISCONNECTED / NETWORK COLLAPSE
                 ↓
NODE CONTINUES 100% UNINTERRUPTED
    ├── Local Sensor Ingestion & Filtering (Resource Engine)
    ├── Local Control Loop Evaluation (Climate / HVAC / Fluid)
    ├── Local Safety Interlocks & Emergency Halt (Operational Matrix)
    ├── Physical Actuator Driver Execution (PWM / Relays / Servos)
    ├── Flash Storage of Committed Parameters (Configuration Core)
    └── Structured Health & Fault Latching (Fault Engine)
```

Every operational node must function safely and autonomously without Hub connectivity. Network loss must never cause erratic actuation, stall a control loop, or bypass a physical safety interlock.

---

# 4. Core Architectural Constitution

## 4.1 Fundamental axiom

> **Every piece of information, behavior, command, resource, and physical action has exactly one authoritative owner and one canonical pathway.**

## 4.2 The four cardinal separations

```text
┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐
│  CONFIGURATION   │   ≠   │  RUNTIME STATE   │   ≠   │     COMMAND      │   ≠   │      EVENT       │
│  Durable Intent  │       │What Is Happening │       │   Instruction    │       │Discrete Incident │
└──────────────────┘       └──────────────────┘       └──────────────────┘       └──────────────────┘
```

1. **Configuration $\neq$ Runtime State:** Configuration expresses durable intent stored in flash (e.g., setpoints, calibration tables). Runtime state represents volatile operating reality (e.g., live temperatures, current RPM, active faults). Live readings or dynamic control targets must never be written to flash configuration.
2. **Command $\neq$ State:** A command is an ephemeral request (`SET_PUMP(ON)`). State is the resulting condition committed by the owning service (`PUMP = RUNNING`). Commands are transient; state is continuous truth.
3. **Event $\neq$ State:** An event is a point-in-time notification of a transition (`TANK_LOW_ALARM`). State is the ongoing status (`LEVEL = 12%`). Consumers must not reconstruct state solely by intercepting event streams.
4. **Logical Policy $\neq$ Driver Mechanics:** Control engines generate unitless or normalized targets ($0\text{--}100\%$). Actuator drivers manage step intervals, PWM duty cycles, attach/detach routines, and settling delays.

## 4.3 Universal physical actuation boundary

```text
Interface / Hub / Controller / Routine
                 ↓
      Logical Command Request
                 ↓
   Validation & Authorization Gate
                 ↓
      Operational Matrix (OM)
                 ↓
     Actuator Domain Manager
                 ↓
     Owning Physical Driver
                 ↓
      Raw Hardware / GPIO / PWM
```

**Prohibition:** No UI, protocol adapter, automation script, scheduler, or control engine may write directly to hardware registers, GPIOs, PWM channels, or relays. All physical actions flow through the dedicated physical driver.

## 4.4 Actuator safety invariant model

When one actuator’s operation creates a mandatory prerequisite for another, that rule must be enforced as a **declarative, hardware-enforced interlock in the Operational Matrix**, not hidden inside application loops:
* *Equalizer:* $\text{FAN\_RUNNING} \implies \text{FLAP\_OPEN}$
* *Tanks Node:* $\text{PUMP\_RUNNING} \implies \text{VALVE\_OPEN} \land \text{TANK\_NOT\_EMPTY}$
* *Thermostat:* $\text{COMPRESSOR\_RUNNING} \implies \text{FAN\_RUNNING} \land \text{SHORT\_CYCLE\_TIMER\_EXPIRED}$

---

# 5. Unified Procedural Matrix

To maintain system integrity, all firmware additions must follow the engineering criteria in this matrix:

| Element | Authoritative Owner | Conceptual Classification | Boundaries & Invariants | Required Validation & Tests |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Variable** | Owning Service / Module | `CONFIG`, `STATE`, `CMD`, `EVENT`, `DERIVED`, `CACHE` | Single writer. Check for duplicate representations. Explicit persistence justification. | Range limits, corruption fallback, default initialization. |
| **Function** | Defined Owning Module | Execution Routine | Single responsibility. Explicit side effects. Document re-entrancy and execution context. | Unit testing of boundary cases; error-code verification. |
| **Command** | Target Owning Service | Semantic Instruction | Routed via Command Manager. Explicit parameters, validation gate, and standard outcome enum. | Parameter range limits, unauthorized source rejection, execution timeout. |
| **Logical Resource** | Resource Engine | Normalized Data Point | Abstracted from hardware. Enforces quality flags, timestamps, and canonical units. | Stale evaluation, disconnect detection, atomic fallback switching. |
| **Sensor Provider** | Hardware Driver / Comms Adapter | Ingestion Provider | Pushes raw data to Resource Engine. Zero control policy. May be swapped without updating consumers. | Noise filtering, loss-of-signal timeout, bad-checksum rejection. |
| **Actuator** | Actuator Domain Manager | Logical / Physical Actuator | Logical 0–100% target. Physical driver owns execution mechanics. Bounded by Operational Matrix. | Command $\to$ Driver $\to$ Hardware path; emergency-stop detach test. |
| **Node Instance** | Instance Config Core | Topology Member | Inherits Node Type. Unique identity. Autonomous local control; Hub independence. | Address collision tests, offline operation, capability advertisement. |
| **Fault** | Fault Engine | Structured Diagnostic | Explicit identity, severity, detection rule, latching policy, and recovery criteria. | Fault injection, latching behavior, auto-clearance, user clearance. |
| **Feature Flag** | Configuration Core | Boolean / Capability Flag | Feature flags must not replace subsystem ownership or serve as ad-hoc global bitmasks. | Compiles out when disabled; runtime disable isolates resources safely. |

---

# 6. Universal Subsystem Engines

All Garuda nodes implement these seven core runtime engines.

```text
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                        UNIVERSAL RUNTIME ENGINES                       │
├──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────────────────┤
│ 1. Resource Engine       │ 2. Operational Matrix   │ 3. Fault Engine   │
│    • Provider Selection  │    • Conjunctive AND    │    • 3 Dimensions │
│    • Freshness Tracking  │    • Severity Hierarchy │    • Latch Engine │
├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
│ 4. Command & Event       │ 5. Flash Persistence    │ 6. Lifecycle      │
│    • Canonical Pipeline  │    • Transactional NVS  │    • Safe Mode    │
│    • Explicit Outcomes   │    • Migration Engine   │    • POST Gating  │
├──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────────────┤
│ 7. Scheduler & Timing Framework (Monotonic vs. Wall-Clock)            │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---

## 6.1 Resource Engine & Data Quality Framework

The Resource Engine decouples control loops from physical sensor hardware and network sources across all nodes.

```text
PHYSICAL / NETWORK PROVIDERS
 Local Sensors (I2C/SPI) │ Modbus RTU │ Remote Node (BLE/ESP-NOW) │ MQTT / Network
                        └─────┬─────┘
                              ▼
                       RESOURCE ENGINE
     ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │ 1. Ingestion & Quality Tagging                  │
     │ 2. Timestamp Tracking (Source vs. Local Receive)│
     │ 3. Canonical Unit Conversion (Kelvin / SI)      │
     │ 4. Provider Eligibility Filter (Fault checks)   │
     │ 5. Arbitration Policy (Freshness-First default) │
     │ 6. Stability Window & Atomic Switch-Back        │
     └────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┘
                              ▼
                  CANONICAL LOGICAL RESOURCE
                     Consumed by Control Loops
```

### Subsystem decisions & operational rules

#### RES-01: Logical Resource Decoupling (`GLOB-32`)
* **The Decision:** Control loops request logical resources (`INTERNAL_TEMPERATURE`, `TANK_PRESSURE`), never physical bus addresses.
* **The Rule:** Providers ingest data and push it into the Resource Engine. Control algorithms consume logical resources with zero knowledge of sensor bus topologies.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** EQ climate loop requests `INTERNAL_TEMP`. Resource Engine resolves this from a local SHT30 sensor and serves $295.15\text{ K}$.
* **Worst-Case Scenario (Tanks):** A local I2C pressure sensor fails on a fuel tank. An operator maps an external Modbus sensor to `TANK_PRESSURE`. The tank control loop continues monitoring pressure without code modifications or re-flashing.

#### RES-02: Eligibility Precedes Arbitration (`GLOB-33`)
* **The Decision:** Candidate providers must pass validity, freshness, and health gates before participating in arbitration.
* **The Rule:** Ineligible candidates (corrupted CRC, stale beyond limits, provider in fault) are filtered out. Arbitration operates only on verified eligible providers.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Two temperature sensors are online. Sensor A has a measurement age of $1.0\text{ s}$; Sensor B has an age of $5.0\text{ s}$. Both are eligible. Freshness-first selects Sensor A.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Sensor A is damaged and transmits corrupt values with a new timestamp ($0.1\text{ s}$ old). Sensor B provides valid readings ($2.0\text{ s}$ old). Sensor A is dropped at the eligibility gate due to CRC failure. Sensor B wins arbitration, preventing bad data from entering the control loop.

#### RES-03: Temporal Provenance Tracking (`GLOB-34`, `GLOB-44`)
* **The Decision:** Sensor readings record both the source measurement timestamp and the local packet receipt timestamp.
* **The Rule:** Measurement age is calculated strictly from the source timestamp. Local receipt time is used only to evaluate transport latency.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An ESP-NOW packet arrives $10\text{ ms}$ after physical measurement. Source time confirms data freshness; local time verifies low network latency.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A network router buffers packets during a Wi-Fi reconnect, delivering a batch of 5-minute-old sensor packets. Local receipt time is current, but source timestamps reveal the data is stale. The Resource Engine marks the readings `STALE`, preventing outdated data from driving active control loops.

#### RES-04: Non-Resetting Measurement Age on Invalid Updates (`GLOB-42`)
* **The Decision:** Receiving an invalid reading preserves the last valid reading up to its expiration limit, but does not reset its measurement age.
* **The Rule:** Retained values age continuously from their original measurement timestamp. An invalid packet cannot refresh data freshness.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A sensor sampled every second drops one packet. The previous reading is retained for that second, keeping the control loop steady.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A probe cable is damaged, generating continuous bus noise. The Resource Engine retains the last valid reading. Because invalid packets do not reset the age counter, the reading ages normally and flags as `EXPIRED` after 10 seconds, safely halting the dependent pump or fan.

#### RES-05: Canonical Unit Normalization (`GLOB-46`)
* **The Decision:** Internal storage and control math use standardized base units: floating-point **Kelvin** for temperature, **Pascals** for pressure, and **Liters** for volume.
* **The Rule:** Normalization occurs at ingestion. Presentation scaling (Celsius, Fahrenheit, PSI, Gallons) occurs strictly at display boundaries.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An imperial probe reports $68^\circ\text{F}$ and a metric probe reports $20^\circ\text{C}$. Both are normalized to $293.15\text{ K}$, allowing direct mathematical comparison.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** In legacy code, subtracting Fahrenheit from Celsius caused math errors that locked cooling loops. Canonical Kelvin math eliminates mixed-unit calculation errors across nodes.

---

## 6.2 Operational Matrix (OM) Framework

The Operational Matrix is a **static declarative rulebook** executed by each subsystem prior to actuation (`GLOB-20`).

### Subsystem decisions & operational rules

#### OPX-01: Component-Local Matrices (`GLOB-36`)
* **The Decision:** Matrices are implemented per physical component, not as a single monolithic system table.
* **The Rule:** Each component owns its matrix:
  * EQ: Fan OM, Flap OM
  * Thermostat: Compressor OM, Fan OM, Reversing Valve OM
  * Tanks: Pump OM, Drain Valve OM
* **Best-Case Scenario:** EQ Fan evaluates the Fan OM before running. Flap state is passed as a boolean predicate.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A change to Tank valve safety rules requires updating the Valve OM. Because matrices are isolated, the Tank pump matrix, EQ matrices, and Thermostat matrices remain unchanged.

#### OPX-02: Conjunctive AND Evaluation (`GLOB-37`)
* **The Decision:** Conditions are evaluated as declarative Boolean equations. Every required predicate must pass for an operation to be permitted:
  $$\text{Permission} = P_1 \land P_2 \land P_3 \dots \land P_n$$
* **The Rule:** If any predicate fails, the permitted path closes, and the matrix returns the outcome assigned to that failure.
* **Best-Case Scenario (Tanks):** Pump start requested. Predicates: Tank not empty (`TRUE`), line valve open (`TRUE`), no overcurrent (`TRUE`), e-stop clear (`TRUE`). Evaluation passes; OM returns `PERMITTED`.
* **Worst-Case Scenario (Tanks):** Pump start requested, but the line valve is closed. The predicate chain evaluates to false; OM returns `PROHIBITED [REASON: VALVE_CLOSED]`, protecting the pump from head-pressure damage.

#### OPX-03: Universal Severity Precedence (`GLOB-39`)
* **The Decision:** Multi-condition evaluations resolve using a universal severity hierarchy:
  $$\text{PERMITTED} < \text{DEGRADED} < \text{UNAVAILABLE} < \text{PROHIBITED}$$
* **The Rule:** Any condition resulting in `PROHIBITED` overrides all `DEGRADED` or `UNAVAILABLE` states.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An optional display is disconnected (`DEGRADED`), while sensor inputs are healthy. The OM returns `DEGRADED`, allowing the node to operate.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An optional sensor is disconnected (`DEGRADED`), while an emergency stop line is cut (`PROHIBITED`). The OM resolves to `PROHIBITED`, shutting down all actuators.

#### OPX-04: Provider Fallback Layer Separation (`GLOB-40`)
* **The Decision:** Provider fallback is handled by the Resource Engine and is not an Operational Matrix outcome.
* **The Rule:** The OM evaluates whether the resulting logical resource is sufficient for the requested operation; it does not track provider switching mechanics.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** Thermostat primary temperature sensor drops out; Resource Engine switches to the backup sensor. The backup provides valid data, so the OM returns `PERMITTED`.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** Both primary and backup temperature sensors fail. The Resource Engine marks the logical resource `UNAVAILABLE`. The OM evaluates the missing resource and returns `UNAVAILABLE`, safely suspending heating.

---

## 6.3 Fault Engine & Global Health Framework

The Fault Engine provides centralized observation, tracking, and diagnostics without acting as an actuator controller (`GLOB-27`).

```text
┌────────────────────┐     ┌────────────────────┐     ┌────────────────────┐
│   OBSERVED HEALTH  │  ≠  │    ACTIVE FAULT    │  ≠  │   RETAINED LATCH   │
│  Current physical  │     │ Classified problem │     │ Explicit safety    │
│    measurement     │     │ meeting thresholds │     │ restriction in NVS │
└────────────────────┘     └────────────────────┘     └────────────────────┘
```

### Subsystem decisions & operational rules

#### FLT-01: Three Independent State Dimensions (`GLOB-26`)
* **The Decision:** System monitoring tracks Observed Health, Active Faults, and Retained Latches as independent dimensions.
* **The Rule:** A healthy physical reading does not clear an active latch. Latching policy is defined per fault type.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An intermittent wiring fault causes transient tachometer drops. The signal normalizes, passes three consecutive evaluation windows, and the active fault flag clears automatically.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A pump suffers a dry-run overheat condition, tripping a latching `PUMP_DRY_RUN` fault. Fluid is added, and fresh level sensors read full. Observed health is healthy, but the latch remains active in NVS, preventing pump restart until a technician validates prime and issues a manual clear command.

#### FLT-02: POST Latch Reconciliation on Boot
* **The Decision:** On boot, fresh POST evaluations reconcile with persisted flash records.
* **The Rule:** Reconciliation logic:
  * Persisted ordinary fault + healthy POST $\implies$ Clear fault.
  * Persisted ordinary fault + failing POST $\implies$ Keep fault active.
  * Persisted Latch + healthy POST $\implies$ **Keep Latch Active**.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A temporary network timeout fault existed prior to reboot. On boot, POST confirms the network link is healthy. The ordinary fault is cleared, and the device enters `READY` mode.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An operator power-cycles a node to clear a safety latch. On boot, POST checks pass, but the startup routine reads the active latch from flash and keeps the device in `SAFE_MODE`.

#### FLT-03: Clear-Fault Override Semantics
* **The Decision:** Executing a `Clear-Fault` command resets the recorded latch state to allow re-evaluation, but does not certify hardware health.
* **The Rule:** Clearing a latch allows the Operational Matrix to re-evaluate operating permissions. If physical fault conditions persist, the Fault Engine re-latches the fault according to configured thresholds.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An operator replaces a blown fuse and executes `Clear-Fault`. The latch clears, the OM permits operation, and the subsystem resumes service.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An operator executes `Clear-Fault` while a motor is still mechanically jammed. The latch clears. The driver attempts a start, detects locked-rotor current over three consecutive cycles, and re-engages the safety latch.

#### FLT-04: Dependency-Based Aggregate Health (`GLOB-31`)
* **The Decision:** Node health is derived from capability dependencies, not a blanket worst-of-all-sensors calculation.
* **The Rule:** Failure of a required dependency marks the capability `UNAVAILABLE`. Failure of an optional dependency marks the node `DEGRADED`.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An EQ operates with an optional ambient light sensor. The light sensor fails. The node reports health as `DEGRADED`, but climate regulation continues running.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A primary fluid level sensor fails on a tank with no backup. Because the level sensor is a required dependency, the tank node marks fluid management `UNAVAILABLE`, safely halting transfer operations.

---

## 6.4 Canonical Command & Event Pipeline

Every external command (Modbus, BLE, HTTP, MQTT, Web UI, Physical Dial) converges onto one internal pipeline (`GLOB-10`).

```text
External Transport / Protocol Adapter
                 ↓
       Input Deserialization
                 ↓
       Parameter Validation
                 ↓
     Operational Matrix Gate
                 ↓
     Owning Subsystem Handler
                 ↓
       State Machine Commit
                 ↓
       Event & Result Emission
```

### Subsystem decisions & operational rules

#### CEP-01: Commit Precedes Event Emission
* **The Decision:** State-change events are emitted only after the internal state has been updated in memory.
* **The Rule:** No event bus notification may be dispatched while a state change is pending or unverified.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A setpoint update is processed. The Configuration Core validates and applies the new value, then emits `SETPOINT_CHANGED`. Subscribers receive the event and read the updated value immediately.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A setpoint change encounters a memory allocation error during application. The transaction aborts. Because events fire only post-commit, no event is emitted, preventing downstream services from acting on a phantom change.

#### CEP-02: Physical User Controls Route via Command Pipeline (`GLOB-41`)
* **The Decision:** Physical user controls (buttons, rotary encoders) issue semantic commands to the Command Manager rather than injecting values into the Resource Engine.
* **The Rule:** Turning a knob generates a `REQUEST_SETPOINT` command. The command passes through range validation, safety gating, and authority checks before any setting changes.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An operator turns a dial. The UI adapter sends a command to increase fan speed. The command is validated, approved by the OM, and applied through the Fan Driver.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A rotary encoder chatters electrically, generating 100 out-of-range pulses while the unit is locked out. The raw inputs generate commands that are rejected at the validation gate, protecting the control loop from erratic behavior.

#### CEP-03: Standard Command Outcomes
* **The Decision:** Commands return an explicit outcome state: `REJECTED`, `BLOCKED`, `PENDING`, `APPLIED`, `FAILED`, or `TIMED_OUT`.
* **The Rule:** Handlers must return an outcome code along with a structured reason code.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An app commands a valve open. The valve moves to position, and the command returns `APPLIED [REASON: SUCCESS]`.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An app commands a valve open while a high-level safety lockout is active. The command returns `BLOCKED [REASON: TANK_OVERFLOW_INTERLOCK]`. The app displays the specific blocking reason to the user.

---

## 6.5 Configuration & Flash Persistence Core

Durable settings are managed through a transactional pipeline that prevents partial writes and flash wear (`GLOB-14`).

```text
Request Change → Validate Complete Transaction → Apply In-Memory → Commit to NVS → Publish Event
```

### Subsystem decisions & operational rules

#### NVS-01: Flash Write Isolation & Debounce Coalescing
* **The Decision:** Routine operational state, dynamic control targets, and live sensor readings are never written to flash.
* **The Rule:** Only validated configuration changes (setpoints, calibration tables, network parameters) are written to NVS. Rapid adjustments (e.g., slider inputs) are debounced in RAM and committed in a single atomic write after input settles.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A user drags a temperature slider through 20 intermediate steps before stopping at $22^\circ\text{C}$. The RAM target updates dynamically while the NVS write debounces for $3\text{ seconds}$, writing to flash only once.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A buggy control loop recalculates fan targets every $500\text{ ms}$. Because control targets are categorized as volatile runtime state, zero flash writes occur, protecting the ESP32 flash memory from premature wear.

#### NVS-02: Recovery from Corrupted Stored Configuration
* **The Decision:** Corrupted NVS configuration triggers fallback to verified factory defaults with diagnostic reporting.
* **The Rule:** All configuration structures carry CRC32 integrity checksums. If a checksum fails at boot, the driver rejects the corrupted block, loads hard-coded factory defaults, logs a `NVS_CRC_CORRUPT` diagnostic, and flags the system for review.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** On boot, configuration blocks pass CRC checks, load into memory, and normal startup proceeds.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An unexpected power cut during a legacy write corrupts flash memory. On the next boot, the CRC check fails. Instead of running with corrupted parameters, the unit loads safe factory defaults ($24^\circ\text{C}$ Auto mode), logs the incident, and remains operational.

#### NVS-03: Explicit Reset Scopes
* **The Decision:** Reset scopes are distinct operations with bounded execution:
  * *Runtime Reboot:* Reloads active flash configuration; clears volatile RAM state.
  * *Config Reset:* Restores user setpoints to factory defaults; preserves calibration and hardware limits.
  * *Calibration Reset:* Restores actuator/sensor calibration to uncalibrated defaults; preserves network and user config.
  * *Factory Wipe:* Clears all configuration, calibration, and network credentials back to factory flash defaults.
* **The Rule:** Executing a configuration reset must never alter calibration tables, and runtime reboots must never clear persistent fault latches.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A user resets forgotten network settings. Wi-Fi credentials reset while the physical fan and flap calibration tables remain intact.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An unauthorized user issues a general reset command to try to bypass a safety latch. The system executes a Config Reset, preserving the active safety latch in NVS.

---

## 6.6 Node Lifecycle & Firmware-Managed Safe Mode

Every node progresses through a standard lifecycle, isolating failures before normal actuation begins.

```text
┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐
│  BOOT / HW   │  →  │ CONFIG LOAD  │  →  │ POST CHECKS  │  →  │ RECONCILE    │
│ Platform Init│     │ Verify CRC32 │     │ Fresh Sensor │     │ NVS Latches  │
└──────────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────┬───────┘
                                                                      │
                         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┴────────┐
                         ▼                                                     ▼
               All Critical Checks Pass                           Critical Dependency Fails
                         │                                                     │
                         ▼                                                     ▼
         ┌───────────────────────────────┐                     ┌───────────────────────────────┐
         │     READY / NORMAL MODE       │                     │          SAFE MODE            │
         │ Full local control authorized │                     │ Actuators disabled & detached │
         │  Degraded if optional missing │                     │ Comms & Diagnostics active    │
         └───────────────────────────────┘                     └───────────────────────────────┘
```

### Subsystem decisions & operational rules

#### LIF-01: Firmware-Managed Safe Mode Isolation
* **The Decision:** Failure of a critical hardware or software dependency during POST transitions the node into a firmware-managed Safe Mode rather than entering an uncontained reboot loop.
* **The Rule:** In Safe Mode:
  1. Actuator drivers are halted and detached.
  2. Climate and fluid control loops are suspended.
  3. Network interfaces, web diagnostics (`/status`), and logging remain active.
  4. The node accepts diagnostic and configuration recovery commands.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** POST verifies all peripherals, sensor buses, and calibration tables. Node enters `READY` mode and begins climate tracking.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** An I2C bus fails during startup, taking down the primary safety sensors. Instead of panic-looping the ESP32, the node enters `SAFE_MODE`. Physical actuators remain safely unpowered, and the web diagnostic interface stays online to report the failed bus to technicians.

#### LIF-02: Progressive Recovery with Bounded Retries
* **The Decision:** Safe Mode implements scheduled, bounded dependency re-tests to recover automatically from transient hardware faults.
* **The Rule:** The node re-tests failed dependencies using exponential backoff. If checks pass and no latches are set, the node transitions to `DEGRADED` or `READY`. Retry attempts are bounded to prevent bus flooding.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** An external sensor module experiences delayed power-up. POST fails on attempt 1, placing the node in Safe Mode. The 10-second re-test detects the initialized sensor, passes validation, and promotes the node to `READY`.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A sensor is permanently damaged. The progressive recovery engine retries at 10s, 30s, 60s, and then caps at 5-minute intervals. The node remains in Safe Mode without saturating the CPU or spamming network logs.

---

## 6.7 Scheduler & Timing Framework

Timing requirements determine which clock reference is used (`GLOB-24`):

```text
┌──────────────────────────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
│            MONOTONIC TIME            │     │           WALL-CLOCK TIME            │
│       Non-decreasing tick count      │     │      UTC / RTC / Network Clock       │
├──────────────────────────────────────┤     ├──────────────────────────────────────┤
│ • Relative durations & delays        │     │ • Human-facing calendar schedules    │
│ • Watchdog timers & retry windows    │     │ • Historical log timestamps          │
│ • Sensor freshness & timeouts        │     │ • Time-of-day automation rules       │
│ • Actuator ramp rates & settling     │     │ • Cross-node timestamp recency       │
└──────────────────────────────────────┘     └──────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### Subsystem decisions & operational rules

#### SCH-01: Monotonic Reference for Durations and Safety
* **The Decision:** Monotonic time is used for all operational safety timing, sensor freshness calculations, and control delays.
* **The Rule:** Network time updates or NTP corrections must never step, pause, or distort monotonic time tracking.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A 10-second fan power-down sequence runs. An NTP sync adjusts the local wall clock forward by 2 hours. The monotonic timer ignores the shift and executes the step after exactly 10 elapsed seconds.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A wall-clock time sync steps the system clock backward by 1 hour during an active pump run. Because duration tracking uses monotonic time, the pump times out normally, preventing an overflow.

#### SCH-02: Scheduler as a Request Generator
* **The Decision:** The Scheduler submits semantic command requests; it has zero direct access to physical actuator drivers.
* **The Rule:** When a scheduled task fires, it builds a command packet and submits it to the Command Manager. The request is evaluated against current safety and operational matrix rules like any external command.
* **Best-Case Scenario:** A calendar task fires at 22:00 requesting `OFF`. The command routes through the Command Manager, updates the mode, and executes a controlled shutdown.
* **Worst-Case Scenario:** A task fires requesting pump operation while an emergency stop is active. The request is submitted, rejected by the Operational Matrix, and marked as `BLOCKED`. The scheduler records the failure without bypassing safety.

---

# 7. Distributed Coordination & Inter-Node Contracts

These contracts govern interactions between the Hub, EQ, Thermostat, and Tanks.

## 7.1 Cross-node data queries (`REP-013`, `REP-014`)

Every node running the Garuda stack implements two standard query contracts:
1. **On-Demand Sensor Query (`REP-013`):** Any node or Hub can query another node for a specific sensor reading. Responses include raw value, canonical unit, quality tag, and source measurement timestamp.
2. **Full Node State Snapshot (`REP-014`):** Nodes provide an on-demand complete state snapshot containing identity, lifecycle state, active configuration, resource qualities, actuator states, active faults, and latches. Used for UI initialization, reconnect reconciliation, and Hub synchronization.

## 7.2 Distributed setpoint synchronization (`GLOB-15`, `CHG-GLOB-002`)

When nodes share environment setpoints (e.g., target temperature shared between Thermostat and EQ):
* Updates carry a **monotonic version sequence and UTC timestamp**.
* Nodes evaluate recency upon receipt; **older updates cannot overwrite newer local settings** (`GLOB-24`).
* Setpoint synchronization is opt-in per node. A node can participate as a local authority or follow distributed updates based on its configuration.

## 7.3 Inter-node HVAC ↔ EQ Compliance Protocol Handshake

The Thermostat and Equalizer coordinate environmental conditioning via this formal protocol:

```text
   THERMOSTAT (HVAC)                                         EQUALIZER (EQ)
           │                                                        │
           │────── Compliance Request (HEAT | COOL) ───────────────>│
           │                                                        │ 1. Evaluate useful delta
           │                                                        │ 2. Check local OM & Safety
           │                                                        │ 3. Check Fan/Flap availability
           │<───── Handshake Response ──────────────────────────────│
           │       (ACCEPTED | NOT_USEFUL | RESOURCE_UNAVAILABLE)   │
           │                                                        │
           │                 [ IF ACCEPTED: ]                       │
           │                                                        │ Purge Cycle Runs
           │<───── Status Polling: PURGING ─────────────────────────│ (Monitored vs. max duration)
           │                                                        │
           │                                                        │ Terminal Condition Reached
           │<───── Terminal Notification ───────────────────────────│ (Equilibrium, Timeout, Hub Loss)
           │       (EQUILIBRIUM_ACHIEVED | TIME_LIMIT_REACHED)      │
           ▼                                                        ▼
```

### Protocol rules
1. **Thermostat Defines Objective:** The Thermostat specifies the goal (`HEAT` or `COOL`).
2. **EQ Determines Local Purge Execution:** The EQ validates environmental usefulness (indoor vs. outdoor differential). If external air cannot assist the target direction, the EQ returns `NOT_USEFUL`.
3. **Acceptance Is a Commitment:** Returning `ACCEPTED` indicates the EQ has verified safety, resources, and delta, and has queued the purge cycle.
4. **Hub-Loss Safety Rule (`CHG-EQ-002`):** Because Compliance is an inter-node coordination contract, active Compliance purge terminates if communication with the Hub or Thermostat times out. The EQ returns to its prior autonomous mode.

---

# 8. Observability, Live Inspection & Testing Framework

## 8.1 Dedicated live status inspection (`/status`)

Nodes with web servers expose a dedicated read-only diagnostic endpoint (e.g., `/status`), separate from any control UI:
* **No Actuation Pathway:** The status page provides visibility only. It contains no controls, inputs, or command endpoints.
* **Separation from Console Logging:** Live inspection streaming operates independently from serial console log levels. Enabling detailed status monitoring does not flood the main debug log.
* **Decision Context:** Where applicable, status fields explain *why* an action occurred (e.g., `FAN_TARGET = 0% [REASON: TEMP_BELOW_EXT_TEMP]`).

## 8.2 Three-tier verification framework

Implementation compliance requires three levels of testing:

```text
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                        3-TIER VERIFICATION MODEL                       │
├──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────────────────┤
│ Tier 1: Unit Tests       │ Tier 2: Integration     │ Tier 3: Hardware  │
│ Host-based compilation.  │ Multi-subsystem tests.  │ Real ESP32 target.│
│ Algorithmic boundaries,  │ Resource Engine to OM,  │ GPIO, PWM, Relays,│
│ range validation, state  │ Command to Driver,      │ Bus timeouts, I2C │
│ machine transitions.     │ POST reconciliation.    │ noise, power cuts.│
└──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────────────┘
```

* **Negative Testing Requirement:** Every subsystem must pass negative testing (e.g., injected sensor failures, corrupted flash, missing network peers, out-of-order commands) before certification.

|---|---|---|---|
| **Stage 0** | **Baseline & Reference Freeze** | **COMPLETE** | Legacy implementation documented; behavioral reference frozen without redesign. |
| **Stage 1** | **Global Architecture Definition** | **COMPLETE** | Ecosystem governance, shared engines, OM model, distributed protocols, decision register. |
| **Stage 2** | **Resource & Provider Engine** | **READY TO START**| Concrete provider interfaces, sensor driver abstractions, buffer allocations. |
| **Stage 3** | **Configuration & Persistence Core** | **RESERVED** | Concrete NVS schemas, atomic commit code, migration routines. |
| **Stage 4** | **State, Events & Faults** | **RESERVED** | Centralized state struct, event bus implementation, fault engine latching. |
| **Stage 5** | **Command Engine & Dispatch** | **RESERVED** | Command Manager, transport deserializers, validation pipelines. |
| **Stage 6** | **Runtime Scheduler & Execution** | **RESERVED** | FreeRTOS task mappings, timer queues, execution budget enforcement. |
| **Stage 7** | **Control Engines & Actuators** | **RESERVED** | Physical driver implementations, PWM generators, ramp steppers, concrete OM code tables. |
| **Stage 8** | **Hardware / Platform Layer** | **RESERVED** | ESPHome platform components, GPIO/bus configurations, HAL mappings. |
| **Stage 9** | **Communication & Protocol Adapters** | **RESERVED** | Modbus RTU, BLE GATT, HTTP REST, MQTT client adapters. |
| **Stage 10** | **Diagnostics, Observability & UI** | **RESERVED** | `/status` web implementation, OLED drivers, metric instrumentation. |
| **Stage 11** | **Validation & Failure Testing** | **RESERVED** | Automated unit tests, integration test runs, hardware-in-the-loop stress testing. |
| **Stage 12** | **Production Integration & Migration**| **RESERVED** | Migration from baseline reference, code deprecation, formal release sign-off. |

# 10. Canonical Global Decision Register

Every global architectural decision is cataloged here with a permanent identifier. Entries are never renumbered or deleted.

```text
                                GLOBAL DECISION REGISTER
┌──────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬────────────┐
│ GLOB-01  │ Wire Contracts Are Transport-Specific                 │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-02  │ Timestamp Encoding Is Transport-Specific              │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-03  │ Request vs. Result vs. State Distinction              │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-04  │ Global Timeout & Deadline Framework                   │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-05  │ Fault Recovery Rules Reside in Operational Matrix     │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-06  │ Universal Communication Loss Framework                │ LOCKED     │

│ GLOB-08  │ Structured Fault Lifecycle (Not Booleans)             │ LOCKED     │

│ GLOB-16  │ Stable Global Network & Node Identity Model           │ LOCKED     │

│ GLOB-21  │ Safety Is Highest Actuator Authority                  │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-22  │ Network Participant Lifecycle States                  │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-23  │ Reconnection State Reconciliation (No Blind Replay)   │ LOCKED     │

│ GLOB-25  │ Machine-Readable Diagnostic Reason Codes              │ LOCKED     │

│ GLOB-27  │ Fault Engine Does Not Own Actuation or Permissions    │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-28  │ Operational Permissions Evaluate Current Conditions   │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-29  │ Operational Matrix Is the Safety Policy Evaluator     │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-30  │ Centralized Fault Engine Owns Health Integration      │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-31  │ Dependency-Based Aggregate Health                     │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-32  │ Resource Engine Owns Logical Ingestion & Resolution   │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-33  │ Provider Eligibility Precedes Arbitration             │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-34  │ Temporal Provenance: Source Time vs. Receive Time     │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-35  │ Derived Resources Are Composable with Cycle Rejection │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-36  │ Component-Local Operational Matrices                  │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-37  │ Conjunctive AND Evaluation for OM Predicates          │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-38  │ Standardized OM Vocabulary (PERMITTED, DEGRADED, ...) │ SPECIALIZED│
│ GLOB-39  │ Universal Severity Hierarchy for OM Outcomes          │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-40  │ FALLBACK Removed from OM Vocabulary (Resource-Only)   │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-41  │ User Input Controls Must Use the Command Pipeline     │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-42  │ Invalid Data Must Not Reset Age of Retained Data      │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-43  │ Three Operational Stages: Fresh, Stale, Expired       │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-44  │ Receipt Time Does Not Prove Observation Freshness     │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-45  │ Global Freshness and Staleness Limits by Default      │ LOCKED     │
│ GLOB-46  │ Kelvin Is the Canonical Internal Temperature Unit     │ LOCKED     │
└──────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴────────────┘
```
